What Constitutes Loyalty. The Rev. H. Ogden on Monarchy and Republicanism.

On Sunday evening the subject of a sermon by the Rev. H. Ogden, pastor of Golborne-street Baptist Church, was" What constitutes loyalty to the throne ?" He took as his text" Render unto Cesar the things that arc Caesar's, and to God the things that are God's." He said that apparently Christ sanctioned monarchies or the rights of rulers. Apparently be sanctioned a tyranny, tor Czesarism was unmitigated despotism. Yes; but when Christ declared the law " Smitten on one cheek, turn the other also," He promulgated a law that made all tyrannies impossible, whether autocratic or democratic. One's thoughts at this moment naturally turned to the visit of our King and Queen. Were monarchies indispensable? Probably an ideal State could dispense with them. Theoretically and ideally an elected repre- sentative would be the most consistent form of the head of the State. But the British Empire was far from ideal. The present congregation was not en ideal one. The pastor of the church was by no means ideal. When they secured that combination they would have a Church of the firstborn. English life had its roots very deep in the past. The most revolutionary of us had a sneaking regard for an ancient life, and when we remembered what progress had been made in English Life for the last eighty years, one was grateful for a constitutional monarchy such as ours. HEAD OF THE STATE REMOVED PROM POLITICAL STRIFE.

The most ardent Republican could not secure a crumb of comfort when he turned to the United States' of America. None would care to ape France. As a nation we were proud that the head of the State was removed from all the political snares that beset the President of any present-day Republic. From a twentieth century point of view, therefore, we " Render unto Cesar the things that are Camar's." But in all rulers there was a Divine right. There is a Divine right of kings as long as kings lasted. Carlyle said it, and Carlyle was always near the soul of things. Charles I. believed it; only he was a baby in an engine room. rriple expansion engines were not toys, nor were engine rooms playgrounds. Great truths and great principles were dangerous when misapplied. Cromwell corrected Charles I. It was drastic correction, but the only correction for such a pupil.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CROMWELL STATUE.

And if one might digress it was a source of great satisfaction that the town possessed a statue of Oliver Cromwell. One watched his transformation clueing these recent days of paint with fear and trembling. The colour now was of the cheapest, but he (the preacher) dreaded lest some vandal should paint it black. There was a Divine right in rulers, and when there was no divine right they should be deposed. Carlyle spoke! of the king as the "Ableman," and in our present king we had the " ableman " for our day and generation. No one claimed for him that he was a genius. He had a great fund of common sense, and that was more valuable to the State than the erratic blunders of a genius. Probably the greatest monarch of the present-day Europe was Kaiser Wilhelmn. He could despatch hasty telegrams, write poems, publish plays, carve marble, plan military manceuvres, lead a line of battleships, and yet one respectfully suggested England wss better with her "ableman." She was safer with her plain King of abundant common sense, and no word on tots subject would be complete without an allusion to the home life of the King and Queen.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
TRY. DOMESTIC LIFE OF ROYALTY.

The domestic life of English royalty was beautifully simple and plain. At the present the Royal hearth- stone was blest with a mother who knew her own mind, and no doubt could assist the King with his, if he should have any doubtful moments. The Queen's quick and i ready sympathy with all distress and need was assisted by a mind that bad been schooled to active and prompt service and we rejoiced that the domestic side of our Royal household was worthy of imitation by all classes. But we must render to God the things that were God's. King George and Queen Mary were subjects of a mightier realm than the British Empire. Their hope of salvation was the same hope as a peasant's hope.

Is Responsibility Under-estimated?

The Rev. Harry Nliddlewood occupied the pulpit at Bold-street on Sunday evening. Choosing as his text the words, " Whoso breaketh a hedge, a serpent shall bite him," Mr. Nliddlewood said no man could escape punishment for his transgression. The penalty must be paid, either directly or indirectly; because, seeing that Conduct made character, it was impossible to disengage himself from the past. Every man was responsible for his own character, and the results of his actions recoiled upon himself. He was utilising for good or for evil the opportunities which were presented for his accept- ance and by his own efforts he would attain his goal and 'determine his destiny. A man was to-day what many yesterdays had made him. There was a modern tendency to under-estimate the responsibility of man. While heredity and environment were tremendous factors in a man's life, he was, nevertheless, possessed of sufficient strength of human will to burst the bonds of sin with God's aid. The gains or losses were ancording to the spirit dominating the man, and his liability to fall only proved his ability to rise. The individualities of responsibility was a great fact of life, and it was possible so to educate the will by making a rirtuous present that a blameless future was ensured. ;very man had a noble conception of life which caused mto pause before committing a sin. Sin was never justifiable. If the hedge was once broken, however, the sin became easier. Occasional indulgence became z. habit, and later, the habit developed into a ruling passion. The only moral, therefore, to be drawn from this was" Respect the hedge, and avoid the bite of the

New Primitive Methodist Minister. Rev. A. J. Chessum's First Sermons at Legh-street Church. The Rev. A. J. Chessum, who succeeds the Rev. W oper, as supei intendant of the Wart ington Primitive Methodist Circuit, inaugurated his ministry at Legh- atreet Church on Sunday. One who is familiar with Chessum's labours at Houghton-le-Spring sends toe following appreciation :—" The Rev. A. J- Chessum is a young man.of more than average intelli- gence and ability. His ministry in all his stations has been very successful, and the stations in which he has travelled have prospered financially, numeri- cally and spiritually. The last four years have been spent in one of the strongest circuits in the North of England, and here he has been highly appreciated. He is blessed with a good voice and uses it with great advantage. He leaves the Houghton-le-Spring Circuit ior Warrington with the prayers and good wishes of all the people." A special reception meeting will be held at Legh-street on July 24th, at which the Rev. . W. Reid (representative of the Free Church Council) will be the principat speaker. In view of this meeting Chessum only touched briefly on his coming to Warrington on Sunday. Large congregations assembled throughout the day and the services were bright and inspiring. The new minister was warmly received, and the whole-heartedness of the welcome should encourage him in his new circuit. Preaching in the morning, Mr. Chessum took for his text, "Ye are the salt of the earth," and drew many analogies from salt as relative to Christian life. salt, he said, had a peculiar and distinguishable flavour, and that was what was required of all Christians. Salt worked quietly, whether seen or not, and yet the people were bound to recognise it. Such should be the life of the Christian, whose influence for good was recognised, even if not perceived. Salt was a universal preservative, and the aim of Christian life was to preserve human life from degeneration. It was also the most universally diffused substance, which was symbolical of Christian influence disseminated throughout the world. They should make their influence felt, and do their utmost to maintain and strengthen the Christian life of the "Ye are the light of the world" was the text from which the rev, gentleman preached an eloquent sermon in the evening. He likened Christianity to the city on the bill, for its attractions lay in the enlivening presence of God ; in the enriching personalities of the saints, and in the enlightening

TIN RI PA LLED The Examiner, Saturday, July 19, 1913.

Ther were weighed before God, not as crowned heads, tatt as good devout souls. Our first allegiance was God, then under God to our present Sovereign. We could indeed say, "God save the King." Latchford Baptist Church. The summer anniversary services in connection with the above church took place on Sunday. The preacher for the day was the Rev. Ambrose Lewis, of Wigan. In the morning be took for his text," Him that is good before God" trona Ecclesiastes ii. 26, and in the even- ing he based his thoughts on the words,"l am the Bread of life," from St. John vi. 35. At both these services, anthems were rendered by the choir. In the afternoon a special service was held at which Mr. W. T. Wilkinson presided, and a cantata entitled "The promises of Jesus" was very ably interpreted by the choir. The Rev. Ambrose Lewis also gave brief addresses on the three sections of the cantata, viz., pardon, peace, and power The collections for the day amounted to nearly £l5.

Rev. T. T. James on "The True Patriotism." Preaching on "The true patriotism" at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday morning, the Rev.T. T. James said that in many touches of human sympathy we had been made to feel that the King himself took eel iously the position which he held. It would take indeed long to tell the story of how we grew into an Empire. It was a story that contained many heroic pages and many sordid ones as well. There was no generalisation possible of the motives which had planted the British flag in so many quarters of the globe. Sometimes it had been the hand of religions persecution which had driven men forth to seek a home where there might be found freedom for the conscience and the sou!. Men sailed to seek God, not gold Sometimes it had been the wild love of danger and adventure, the restlessness of activity, the sheer irre- pressible spirit of daring which had urged men to nn- known lands to make fresh outposts and new settlements. And sometimes the story of our national expansion had been the grossest selfishness, the unlovely lust of gold, the shameless greed of avarice. From many a motive, sometimes good and true, sometimes frankly mean and worthless, the great Empire under which we live had been built throughout the years. THE GRAVITY OF OUR HUMAN PROBLEM. How easy it was to miss the real gravity of our human problem in the overpowering sense of the magnitude of our material possessions ! Yet because men were more than machinery, and personalities were more than property, and souls were more than square miles of territory, we must set down quite honestly what all this external glory meant or did not mean in the realm human character and moral worth, and in liberty and freedom and fulness of life. How true it often was that the mere outward splendour blinded us to the deepest needs, and the romance of far distant fields of imperial possession obscured the appalling problems in our very midst ! Here, while the tide of conquest rolls Against the distant golden shore, The starved and stunted human souls Are with us more and more. Or take the great misslonnry obligation which was laid upon us, the undeniable duty of sharing whatever we possessed of light and truth with those whose vision is limited, and whose religious faith is poor and ill-informed. There was a day when we were urged to this task on the ground that in far distant lands myriad of our fellow- beings would be doomed to eternal perdition because they had never heard of Jesus Christ. We could believe it no longer, and we did not believe it. He supposed it might have been possible for men to believe it once. Yet the missionary obligation had not become less binding. It was a poor result of a broader conception of the love of God to allow our own love and service to evaporate. If the supremacy of Christ was a reality to us, the claim that we should take to others what was supreme truth to ourselves was as insistent as ever. We needed perpetually to be in fear not so much of what would happen to blind, stumbling races because knowledge was withheld from them, but of the spiritual poverty and sonl emptiness which must overtake ourselves if we shirked the call to circle the earth with the love of God, and kept to ourselves in idle selfishness the treasure we were meant to hold in common with the race.

THE ROYAL TOUR IN LANCASHIRE.

End of a Successful and Eventful Progress.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THEIR MAJESTIES "PROFOUNDLY IMPRESSED."

The Royal visit to Lancashire terminated on Monday with a State visit to Manchester and Salford, which in several particulars, though not in spectacular magnificence, closely resembled the progress through Liverpool on Friday of last week. Nine miles of thronged thoroughfares were traversed by their Majesties in an open carriage, and addresses were received from the Corpora- tion, the University, and the Chamber or Commerce, and replied to by the King. The Lord Mayor of Manchester (Mr. S. W. Royse) was knighted. In the afternoon their Majesties left in the Royal train for the Metropolis.

DID NOT SEE THEIR MAJESTIES.

Guardian Complains of Treatment of

Workhouse Children.

At the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday, it was reported that the manager of the Picturediome, through Miss Broad- bent, invited the children at the Workhouse to witness the exhibition of pictures of the Royal visit te Warrington. Advantage was taken of the privilege and cordial thanks were now expressed to the manager for his kind invitation. Mr. W. C. Bnooxs said it was pleasing to know that the children had had an opportunity ,: seeing cinematograph pictures of the Royal visit. At the same time, he deeply regretted that they were not allowed to see the King and Queen in person. They were kept in the institution all dsy, and the officers along with them—a very unsatisfactory thing which had _never been known before.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
"IF I WFRE

How the Rev. A. M. Mitchell

Would Rule Us

===== 0002601_19130719_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
CRUSADE AGAINST SLUMS.

Under the heading "If I were King," the Rev. A. M. Mitchell has come characteristic comments in the " Burtonwood Parish Magazine" on the visit of the King and Queen to Lancashire. He writes : SHAM AND HUMBUG.

"If I were King" I would forbid all civic receptions until all slums and purlieus are entirely swept away. Think of all the sham, all the humbug, all.the fric- tion, all the rivalry, all the jealousy, ad the heart- burning which lie behind these shows and pageants in honour of royal visits—the cost, which is enormous, is quite a secondary matter £3OO per minute is not worth mentioning. Our King's impressions of our towns must be very wrong, indeed, when he sees. them only in holiday attire, gaily decorated in his honour—when, again, he is taken only through leading streets, where he really beholds a transformation scene which lasts for a day, or less, which, when it vanishes leaves things more sordid and dismal than ever. Behind all these gaily and costly decorated streets,squares, parks, city and town halls lie unholy slums, foeted alleys, where dwell, without option, with their parents the children of the streets, ragged, unkempt, unfed, con- demned to hell on earth, crushed beneath the iron heel of a cruel despotism of wealth misapplied, of power abused.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
CIVIC DECEPTION.

"If I were King" I would insist—"take these decora- tions hence, remove these symbols of loyalty, these expensive geegaws—let me get behind this civic show, this civic deception, this civic profligacy to the "Ghetto "of your town. Our Father's house—this glorious world—is far the health, the happiness, the joy of all; but we have made it a den of poverty, a sink of disease, an inferno of squalor." To speak thus might be deemed unconstitutional,yet would not it be true and right, would not it be merciful and Just? Our much- loved King, deservedly beloved can have no real conception of the misery prevalent in our Lancashire towns when he makes his semi-State progress through the stage-managed streets, which hide away the truth. Ring down the curtain.

"WHITED SEPULCHRES" ON ROYAL DAYS

Of Rochdale it is reported the route of the proces- sion through the town is marred by an unsightly patch of property. The Royal visitors instead of seeing Wheelpit Court, a dismal alley containing three white- washed cottages here view a handsome background of cloth bearing a welcome to the Sovereign and his Consort. One never knows, as a mere on-looker, what lies behind these much bedizened stage-managed streets. A King would be a King indeed who would command his procession to turn aside from the quarters gay with bunting and lavishly festooned, to the parts where are housed (?) the poorest of the poor, that he their Kingly brother might see them as they are daily to be seen, in the dishabille, that he might understand the real life of these wretched victims of a cruel civilisation—a decadent Christianity. Our cities and towns on Royal days are whiter' sepulchres; the royal route is handsome, beautiful, above all costly; but beyond the zone of decoration is poverty and misery, disease, and death. ' "If I were King" my next request would be: "Take me to your poorhouse, to your Union Infirmary, your Cottage Homes; let me see and know how you feed, clothe, and house the people whom misfortune, poverty, sickness, or brutality have deprived of their glorious liberty; let me see and know for my own self that the helpless ones are well cared for and happy; let me assure myself that our State children are well housed, properly fed, and wisely educated; let me clasp the hand of brother; let me lay my hand in sympathy and blessing up.)n the heads of the poor shorn lambs of adversity."

===== 0002601_19130719_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
LOYALTY AND "DOCKED WAGES."

"If I were ring" no worker should lose a day's pay—no, not half a day through my royal visitation. Loyalty is very noble, splendid, which can stand the strain of " docked " wages because of the coming of the King. Wage shortage to the tune of two or three days' play" is a heavy penalty at any time to the toiler—a royal visit is no exception: it does not relieve, but rather accentuates the strain. A time of conviviality to the wealthy employer may prove a time of privation to the artisan and labourer. Enforced holidays should be paid for at ordinary wage rates, at. least ; for struggling families to have, may be, one-half of the otdinary weekly income cut off is a• sure and certain road to disaffection and disloyalty. "If I were King" the royal proclamation should stipulate for no inter- ruption of work, or, if a holiday is observed, that every worker male and female shall receive at least the full average daily wage. It is easy to grant holidays which cost us nothing, which mean, it may be, the saving of our business pockets—nothing easier. A kingly King, like our own King George, desires the toilers and the poor to benefit, not to suffer, by his personal visitation of our industrial centres.

WOULD ABDICATE FOR WOMAN SUFFRAGE.

"If I were King " I would abdicate, if necessary, in favour of Women's Suffrage. I would lay aside my kingly crown, throw off my royal robes, and stand forth a king indeed—but a King without a throne. "If I were King" I would not govern without my women subjects —their voice, their vote, their wisdom, their judgment should be to me of equal value with the Parliament of men.

Women are like men in this—they err—they make mistakes; they transgress even as Cabinet ministers and Kings themselves have erred in the past, and may transgress in the present—but errors and trans- gressions among either sex do not destroy their rights as citizens, nor set aside) their claims. A Parliament would speak with greater wisdom, with more authority if it expressed the womanly sentiment and judgment equally with the manly. Have not the " erring " ones among the women, at least, proved their sincerity, and, also their determina- tion, their unalterable resolve to win the vote, to assert the Scriptural principle of the equality of the sexes ? What GGvernment can honestly remain in power

===== 0002601_19130719_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE KING'S LETTER.

The King has addressed the following etter to Lord Derby : Buckingham Palace, July 14th, 1913. My Dear Lord Derby,---.Now that the visit has ended which, through your kindness, the Queen and I have been able to pay to practically the whole of the industrial districts of Lancashire, I wish to tell you how profoundly impressed we have been by the experiences of these stirring days. In the great cities of Liverpool and Manchester, in the many trade centres on the high roads of the country districts traversed in our motor journeys of some 230 miles, we have received a spontaneous and, indeed, affectionate welcome. Not only were the streets gaily and tastefully decorated, but almost every house throughout . our progress bore signs of its share in this general manifestation of loyalty and goodwill, which was most gratifying to us. THE CHILDREN'S MERRY VOICES. During the week we have had the pleasure of seeing thousands of children, we have heard their merry voices, have listened with delight to their singing, to their hearty cheers, and have watched with joy their happy faces. In Liverpool the displays of 2,000 children from the city schools was a remarkable demonstration of physical training and discipline, reflecting the highest credit both on instructors and pupils. The various mills and factories which we visited afforded an insight into the life of the dense populations of this home of world-wide commerce, and we appreciated the cordial greet- ings extended to us from the employes as we passed through the workshops and buildings decorated in our honour. The vast concourse of merchant vessels in the Mersey reminded us of the wealth and greatness of the mercantile marine, and it fittingly con- cluded with the opening of a dock to contain the biggest ships now building or ever contemplated. The dignity and devotion of the service and the beautiful singing of the massed choirs could not but impress all who assisted in the ceremony. TERRITORIALS' ZEAL AND KEENNESS. I was pleased to notice the zeal and keenness with which the Territorials had paraded in the different towns, and the general bearing of the " troops of the West Lancashire Division which marched past me after church parade on Sunday was very creditable. The various boys' organisa- tions were well represented in the different localities, and it was a pleasure to see on all occasions strong contingents of the National Reserve, but especially in the march past at Liverpool and the muster in Platt Fields, Man- chester. I particularly remarked the constant attendance of the doctors, nurses, and men of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, and their prompt attendance to any call. Please express to the Lord Mayors of Liverpool and Manchester, to the Mayors, to the Chairmen of the County Council and of the Urban and District Councils, my admiration of the manner in which the arrangements bad been planned, and the remarkable precision with which all the details were carried out, enabling us to fulfil a long and varied daily programme while adhering punctually to the stated hours. PRAISE FOR THE POLICE. The police duties must have been difficult and exacting, but the cheerful, willing demeanour of the enormous crowds no doubt rendered these duties less onerous than might have been expec- ted. I know how much is due to Mr. Lane, the Chief Constable of the county. For his personal services to the Queen and myself we are specially indebted. The men under his command, as also the Chief Constables and police of the boroughs are deserving of much praise for the manner in which they co-operated in doing their work with efficiency and tact. Lastly, I thank you most sincerely for the endless, untiring pains and trouble which you have for upwards of a year bestowed upon the preparations for everything connected with this visit. and I cordially con- gratulate you upon the unqualified success of your admirable organisation. "A REAL AND HOMELY RELATION" BETWEEN

KING AND PEOPLE

The warm-hearted manner in which we have been greeted by all classes, young and old, has touched us more than I can say. We hope that our coming amongst the people of the County Palatine may bring us nearer to their hearts, and that they may ever regard the ancient title" Duke of Lancaster," of which I am the proud bearer, not as a purely historical distinction, but as the symbol of a real and homely relation strengthen- ing the bond of union between them and their Sovereign.__ .

—Believe me very sincerely yours, (Signed) GEORGE R.I.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE DUKE OF LANCASTER."

Lord Derby has also received the following further letter from his Majesty's private secretary :

Knowsley, 14th July, 1913. Dear Lord Derby,—The King hopes that on future occasions, when his health is drunk in Lancashire, the wording of the toast may be the "Duke of Lancaster." In commemoration of the Royal visit and of the part taken by the children in the welcome accorded to their Majesties, the King trusts it may be possible to grant an addition to their usual summer holidays. Yours very truly, (Signed) STAMFORDHAM.

LORD DERBY TO THE MAYOR.

The Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph) has received the following message of thanks from Lord Derby : Derby House, Stratford Place, W. _ 15th July, 1913.

Dear Mr. Mayor, You will have seen published in the public Press a letter that his Majesty the King has been graciously pleased to write to me dealing with his tour in Lancashire. I feel, however, it is only courteous to y( u to send you direct a copy of the letter, for its contents affect you as much as they affect me,and bear testimony to his Majesty's grateful appreciation of your efforts to make the tour a success. May I, on my own behalf, thank you most sin- cerely for the courtesy you bare shown to me and the consideration you always gave to any suggestion on my part, for which I shall always be profoundly grateful.—Yours sincerely. DERBY. His Worship the Mayor of Warrington. -- KING AND MESSRS. CROSFIELD.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Children Who Will Not Eat Seed Cake.

The fortnightly meeting of the Malcom Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesatty,Ar. Linnher, Jun., presiding. TO DEAL WITH DISSOLUTE WMIEN.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 19, 1913. FARM AND GARDEN. I"A PATHETIC FIGURE."

STRIKE ENDED.

---- Satisfactory Settlement Arranged.

The final stage of the settlement of the West Lanca- shire farm dispute was reached on Saturday evening, when a special meeting of the Liverpool District Farmers' Club was held in St. Martin's Hall to confirm the action of the joint committee of farmers and labourers, who had arranged the telly's of settlement reached at Ormskirk on Monday of last week. Mr. S. Hurst, chairman of the club, presided, supported by Mr. S. T. Rosbotham, chairman of the Ormskirk branch of the Lancashire Fat mers' Associa- tion ; Mr. F. U. Blundell, J.P., C. 0., Crosby Hall; Mr. J. Ellis Potter, Moss Hall, Aughton ; and practi- cally all the representative farmers in the Liverpool and Orniskirk districts. In all there would be between 200 and 300 farmers present. The proceedings were conducted in private, but the following statement was made to the Press by the chairman :

" Mr. Hurst stated that Mr. Ro3botham and him- self, as two members of the joint committee, outlined the proceedings which had led up to the settlement, winch practically means that all questions arising out of the dispute have to be considered by a joint com- mittee of six farmers and six labourers, with an inde- pendent chairman agreed upon by bpth parties. He also said that the terms of settlement had been approved on Thursday last at Ormskirk by the Lanca- shire Farmers' Asseciation,and he hoped the members of their club would do the same, so that the committee could get to work at once. "Mr. Ro3bothain also spoke, urging the acceptance of the terms.

"A full discussion then took place, after which it was unanimously resolved that the settlement be approved." Meanwhile, all the men are practically now at work on their old firms, and no serious cases of victimisa- tion are expected to arise.

CORONER AS CHAIRMAN OF CONCILIATION COMMITTEE.

Mr. S. Brighouse, the ccunty coroner, has been elected unanimously chairman of the joint committee of six reprt sentative West Lancashire farmers and six farm labourers appointed by masters and men to inquire into all grievances arising out of the strike, which ended last week.

Various r ames had been suggested, but when that of Mr. Brighouse was put forward it was accepted by both pasties, who felt that his local knowledge of everything r ertaining to West Lancashire would prove a rare asset in his position as independent chairman.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE OF THUNDERSTORMS.

The fine response made in the areas visited recently by thunderstorms has, says the" Times, " excited the envy of the fatmeis whose land escaped the down- pours. Two years ago, when prolonged drought wrought havoc with the root crops, it was stated that in Norfolk the trail of a severe thunderstoim in July could be traced by ths appearance of the green crops throughout the rest of the season. The local deluges in the week bebre last, mostly in the eastern counties, have resulted in similar inequalities in the condition of the crops. The lines of demarcation are not so pro- nounced as in 1911, the downponrs having been less concentrated, but the difference between the centre of the favoured areas and the localities outside the range of the storms is sufficient proof of the value to the farmer of June rains. The effect of the drought is revealed in the stunted appearance of spring corn, green crops, and pastures. The wheat, which is independent of summer rains, promises bell, but barley and oats vary widely, with light croi s pre- dominating, and it is doubtful if after midsummer this state of things can be altered.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
RABBITS ARE GAME.

'Barrister," replying to a correspondent in " Farm and Home," says Yes, rabbits are game, and you have got hold of an idea which is very common, but none the h-ss wrong. You are not entitled to invite anybody to come for a day's rabbit shooting on your farm. The landlord retains the sporting rights, and that means that he retains for himself the right of shooting all game, furred and feathered, on thci land, but the Ground Game Act gives you and the occupier a right to shoot ground game, hares and rabbits, and this right your landlord cannot take from you, and you cannot even take it from yourself, because any agreement by which you purport to give up your rights as occupier in respect of the destruction of ground game is null and void. The law does not allow you, however, to shoot rabbits and hares without having either a gun licence or a game licence, and even if you employ a man and pay him to hoot, the rabbits you must provide him with a gun licrnce, but as regards inviting a friend to come for a day's shooting, the Ground Gawe Act does not remit you to do this, and if you invite a friend a gun licence is rot enough for him—ho most have a game licence, and even then he will be liable to be dealt with as a trespasser in pur- suit of game, because the authority given to you under the Ground Game Act has been exceeded, and your landlord can deal with your friend as a trespasser in pursuit of game. Rabbits are game, and the idea that they are not because a farmer can shoot them when he only has a gun licence is wrong; that is a statutory provision applying only to farmers and occupiers and not to ether periple.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
FOXES AS RATTERS.

While passing along a street in Leicester the other day I was very interested in seeing a man carrying a fox uncier his arm, and attached to his coat by a chain. I found that his name was J. Gaunt, of Ambergate, Derbyshire, that he was a ratcatcher, and used the fox to assist him in his work, This one was two months old, but. he informed me that he always used foxes for this purpose. During the last twenty years he had trained six for this use, and had found them quite as efficient as the terriers, which they assist in killing the rats after they have been bolted by the ferrets. I do not know whether the use of foxes for this purpose is at all general, but it is the first time I have ever heard of them being used for such.—" Country Life."

===== 0002601_19130719_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM WORK FOR SCHOOLBOYS.

In a report recently issued by the Rural Education Conference the opinion is expressed that if more farmers gave employment, combined with explanatory instruction, on their farms to boys during the school holidays, and paid them a suitable wage for their work, the interest which boys now take in farm work when they leave school would be very greatly increased, and the tendency for them to seek employment in the town rather than remain in the country would be materially checked. The conference recommended all local education authorities to provide instruction ,in manual prccesses for young farmers and agricul- tural labouters.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

WATERING SWEET PEAS.—There is a great difference of opinion as to the value of watering sweet peas during dry weather, but we think all good growers are agreed that a light overhead syriuging or hosing with clear water during the evenings of hot days is beneficial to the plants. This not only washes away dust and other injurious accumulations, but puts a decided cheek to the ravages of insects.—" The Garden."

Homa.—Too much emphasis cannot be put on the necessity of the free use of the hoe among all grow- ing crops, and if anyone is sceptical as to its merits just hoe between a few rows of Brassicas once each week and leave a few rows untouched and the differ- ence in growth will be quite apparent at the end of a month, especially if the weather is dry.—" Agricul- tural Economist and Horticultural Review." PRUNING CLIM RING ROSES.-- A few of the earliest flowering varieties are over, and those that are not likely to produce a second crop of bloom, such as tea rambler, polyantha simplex and carmine pillar, may have the old flowering wood cut out, thus giving the young growth every chance of developing properly. This early pruning may not commend itself to those who like to see their arches and pillars well clothed all the summer, but the results obtained from such treatment are good enough to warrant it being done, and it is really astonishing how quickly the young growths develop.—" The Clarden." Mr. Ramsay Macdonald Ridicules Lord Wolmer.

THE TRADE UNIONS ACT.

The annual Labour demonstration in c -nnection with the Newton Division Parliamentaiy Labour party was held on Saturday week at Hasdock Park Racecourse. The day was favourable, and the muster at Ashton-in- Makerfield wa4 larger than usual. At the racecourse the concourse took seats on the grandstand for the speechmaking. Mr. W. Speakman, of Earlestown, presided, and said that before the next election they hoped to see the plural voter abolished. In that division there was an actual majority of the electorate in favour of the Labour candidate, but under the operation of plural voting outsiders might come in and deflect the true position. However, they hoped to change all that shortly. PLAYING WITH LOADED DICE.

Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, M.P., said he did not object to Lord Wollner being in the House of Commons, he served a great use there. (Laughter.) He embodied in himself in an exceedingly peculiar and dramatic way the ignorance and offensiveness of the class to which he belonged. He was, therefore, very sorry that this week he introduced a Bill which would compel him in future to tell the truth. (Laughter.) Politicians delighted in murdering their opponents, but it was very rare to see a man so honest and so simple-minded as to introduce a Bill which meant his own political suicide. He had been told that it was only a joke, but it was poor humour that needed a public explanation. Th, y said that Mr. Seddon would have been their member if they alone had been the voters. That was quite true, and all the week, when they had been fighting on committee trying to get the Plural Voting Bill through. his mind had bcen on constituencies like that. Why should the work- people of Newton be thwarted and misrepresented becAuse a thousand odd property owners in the borough of St. Helens were able to vote in Newton as well as in St. Helens ? That was playing with loaded dice, and in so far as the Labour party was concerned they would do everything they could to secure that when the next election came Newton would ho voted upon by Newton electors—(applause)—and if they then liked to return L'ird Wolmer they must take the consequences.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
"A PATHETIC FIGURE."

He appealed for them to always be sympathetic for him. He could not help it. (Laughter.) There was no man who regarded Lord Wolnter with a more tender eye and a more sympathetic thought than he did. He wished they had been with him in the committee that dealt with the Trades Unions Act.

He was there, and they would have been moved to Fity if they had seen the poor, pathetic figure of their member t ying first d all to draft amendments that were in order, and, finding that he could not do it, then trying to draft amendments that made common sense, and finding he could not do it; trying to draft amendaients upon which divisions could be taken, and finding he could not do it : doing nothing but putting things on the paper that the chairman explained should never have been there ; trying to raise points which had never been embraced in the resolutions he put down. That pathetic figure raised every sentiment of pity in his mind. (Laughter.) It got so bad at last that they had to send after the second or third day a lawyer to tly to help him to put up a really decent fight. Things then became a bit better, but sill they had to list n to half-bouts of nervous, jumping, stammering, confused speeches about trade unionism, statements that he did not believe in political.action by trade unions or in this, that, or the other thing about trade unions, but still he was a good, sound trade unionist all the same. (Laughter.) As a matter of fact,if they had paid Lord Wollner to show what their enemies were, they really would have had value for their money. Therefore, he was bound to say that though he was a Labour member, and wanted the Labour party increasing in the House of Commons, he was bound to say that the disappearance of Lord Wolmer would be a great loss to the party in the house. (Applause.) But they must remember that they must look after themselves, and that they could not afford to be generous in order to supp'y the house with an object of amusement and commiseration. Mr. J. A. Seddon made an appeal for the farm labourers. The fight was not over ; it was only just beginning. A fortnight ago the labourers awoke from the sleep of centuries,stung by the very wretched- ness of their lives, encouraged by the success of other workers, they at last said they would not accept the cmditions under which they laboured.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

PARENTS' RESPONSIBILITY.

There was a sequel to the terrible burning fatality which occurred at Halton, near Unicorn, a few weeks ago, when William Henry Miller, the father of the deco ised child, WI'S summoned at. Runcorn on Monday for allowing his child to be in a room where there was an unprotected fire. Superintendent linnion said the defendant was summoned under the Children Act. This was the third case in the district. On the 2lth of last month the defendant went to his work, and his wife journeyed with a trip to Chester, leaving the child three and a half years of age in care of a ten-year-old girl. The child's clothing caught fire, and it was terribly burned, and it subsequently died in the Runcorn Cottage Hospital. Since the occurrence the defendant had secured a fireguard. Defendant was bound over under the First Offenders' Act in the EMU of 40s. to be of good behaviour for three years.

JUDGE STANGER'S TEST. COMPENSATION CLAIM AT RUNCORN.

Judge York Stanger heard at Runcorn County Courts on Tuesday, an application for compensation by Edward Antrobus against the Helsby Insi fated Cable Company, Mr. H. N. Linaker, for the applicant., said that while his client and some other men were layine an electric cable on November 16, 1911, a heavy stone fitg fell on his right foot. The main bone in the foot was frac- tured, and the nerver and the soft parts were also injured. Antrobus was off work for five m9ntha, when he was found light work by the company at Frodsbam. lie wa3 subsequently tranvferred to the storis at Runcorn, where he worked until Christmas last year, when he was paid off with a numhe: of other workmen. He claimed compensation from that date. The applicant said he could walk only on his heel. He suffered great pain, and he would not be able to work again. After hearing medical evidence, the Judge ordered the man to his room in order that he might view the foot. After a consultation with the doctors, the Judge said that from what he had seen his judgment would he for the respondents,as he considered that the incapacity due to the accident had ceased. Costs and quallf3ing fees were also awarded on Scale B. AGED MAN'S DESPAIR.

When pulled out of the Widnes Canal by a youth named Hatton, Joseph Harrison, Albert-road, en old man of seventy-seven years, said to his rescuer, " Why did you not leave me in ? lam in great pain." He was brought before the Widnes Court, on Monday morning, and Superintendent Foster stet( d than he was charged with attempting to commit suicide, and also with being a person whose mind was unsound. On the sth of July he threw himself into the canal near Sullivan's works, and was .got out promptly by Richard Hatton.

The rharge of attempting to take his life was with- drawn, the Superintendent stating that the man would hz, better under supervision at the Workhotrie, because he repeatedly told the officer who brought him to the station that he wished he was dead.

Dr. Hutchinson said he was of opinion that the man was not fit to be at large. Asked if he had anything to say, the old man replied, "I would sooner go to the lads for a week."

Superintendent Foster : I won't take the responsi- bility of his going to relatives. The Bench ordered Harrison to the workhouse for fourteen days for an examination into the state of his mind.

An upward cut should be made about an inch in length, starting the cut nearest the main stem and cutting through a joint pointing away from the plant ; a small stone should then be placed in the incision to keep the cut open. A CHARMING BARDY ANNUAL—One of the most beautiful hardy annuals that we have flowering just now is Clarkia elegans salmon queen. This is grown as a broad edging to the kitchen garden path and forms almost a low hedge, the plants being about eighteen inches high. The double flowers, which are very freely produced, are a delightful shade of salmon piak. Near by we have scarlet queen, , the flowers of which are salmon scarlet in colour. These two Clark ias ought to be grown wherever hardy flowers are appre- ciated. —" The Garden,"

Advantages of Voluntary System For Island Nation.

WARRINGTON AND INSURANCE.

The remarkable hold which progressive politics have on the minds of the workers is exemplified by the largo crowds which gather at the weetily open-air meetings promoted by the Progressive Labour League. On Aionday evening there was another excellent attendance at Bridge Foot, when Mr. Thomas Ternent, of Rochdale, vas the principal sp‘...aker.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
TARLFF REFORMERS AND FOOD PRICES.

Mr. JOSEPH ELwELL, who presided, said he under- stood that some of their Tariff Reform friends,who held a meeting in Warrington last week, weie making:great complaints about the rise in the price of food during the past few years. Why they should bring that charge against the Liberal Government he did not know. The main causes which influenced the prices of food or any other commodity were : firstly, the law of supply and demand ; secondly, the coot of production, and placing the article upon the market. He was convinced that no legislation of the present Government had tended eitner to reduce the supply or in any way to increase the cost of production without also giving the workers the benefit of increased wages through improved trade. Nothing had been done by the Government to limit or restrict trade in any shape or form. (Applause.) it was a peculiar fact that the Conserva- tives, when in office, strongly repudiated the idea that increases in food priced were due to their legisla- tion. '[ho Hon. Mr. Egerton, who, at that time, repre- sented the Knutsford division, enunciated that idea, and said the advance was due to the operation of the law of supply and demand. Yet, when the Lib2rals were in power, their opponents charged them with the responsibility for the enhanced cost of living.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
WAGES AND COST OF PRODUCTION.

If the Liberals had imposed taxes, as the Tariff Retormers were advocating last week, there would have been an element of • truth in the accus.ition, but they had refrained from levying any taxes of that kind. It was worth noting that hi 1900 wages were up, but for several years afterwards their tendency was downward, while the cost of food increased. 6ince the Liberals had been in power there had been an almost continuous increase in tue wages earned in most of the great trades. (Applause.) The miners were being better paid than formerly, with the result that coal was dearer. The ironworkers were getting better wags and iron was consequently dearer. Farmers had to pay more for their implements, and so the effect spread. If men got higher wages the cost of production increased, and the article necessarily increased in value. The workers were, however, earning little wages to pay for the goods they required, and it was clear that the Govern- ment had not caused the consumer to sutler ill-effects by legislative action.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
SECURITY FOR TRADE UNIONS.

The Government's whole policy had been one of pro- gressive legislation largely, though not solely, in the interests of the worsere. (Applause.) When the Government came into office the position of trade unionists were so precarious that no trade organisation could say that its funds were safe. Those disabilities had been remedied by the Trades Disputes Act. (Appl3use.) The Government had also passed old ago pensions and national insurance—(applause)—and a further instalment of insurance was promised in the the interests cf those who vveie most in need of it— the aged'. (Applause.) The casual corker would also be helped, end the workman who was out of work through slackness of trade would not be required to pay the full amount of 7d. per week, but only nis own contribution of 4d. (Applause.) TORIES AND CONSCRIPTION.

Mr. TERSEST claimed that they bed every reason to be proud of what the Liberals had accomplished during their seven and a half years of office. (Hear, hear.) They had achieved one notable record, tor never before in toe history of our country had we teen at peace with Europe arid the rest of the world for seven and a half years. (Applause.) Everyone in the country benefited from a peace policy, sod if anyone was hit by the disaster of war, it was the wage•earning section of the community. They had at the head of foreign affairs one of the ablest statesmen and geniuses who bad ever held office. Sir Edward Grey—(applause)—stood head and shoulders above every probable success.r in the Tory party at tle present time. If the Tories were returned to plwer they would encourage a bastard imperialism ad jingoism. They deliberately told us that they were going to introduce %tat they called National Service, wnich wus conscription under another name. The voluntary system had served us well at, trying times in our rational history, and it would suffice in the future. Ihere must be no ieturn to the days of the old press gang. At the time of the Peninsular war the Militia Act was in force, and men were compelled by ballot to serve in that campaign. 'I he Duke of Wellington, however, wrote to the War 011 ice and said he did not want thxt class of men, but volunteers. (Applause.) As an island nation we depended for our first line of defence upon the navy, and, under'Lib.ralistn, we had a strong, efficient navy.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
"A MAD SCHEME."

In 1901, the Tory party reduced the navy estimates by £3,000,0b0 in order that they might give that money to their triends, the landowners. Under Liberalism, more money was spent on the navy, and within the List three months the " Times" had made the statement that the British navy had never been so strong or efficient as at present. (Applause.) Our first line of defence was impregnable. If the Germans contemplated invasion, they would have to smash our navy as a preliminary, end even then they would not think of landing, but would starve us into submission. A conscript army was just as liable to starve as a civilian or any other tome. l'he policy of conscription was a mad scheme, especi- ally in view of the fact the whole trend and spirit of the age was,sgainst war. 'The Tories could not see that," but," commented the speaker, " that is why men are Torits—simply because they have no vision and cannot read the signs of the times." TIIE LORDS AND HOME RULE.

The Government had taken away a barrier to pro- gress in limiting the veto of the House of Lords, hut, even under the new conditions, they were unfairly handicapped. They had caught the tiger, cut its claws, and extracted its teeth, but it still had a little power and it was making full use of it. In the House of Lords that day Lord Lansdowne was moving a resolu- tion to the effect that the Home Rule Bill ought to be referred to the country. Would Lord Lansdowne and Mr. Boner Law give a pledge that, if an election were fought next week, it would be confined to the one issue of Home Rule? They dare not give such a pledge, Le,:muse they would endeavour to aivert the attention of the electorate to Marconi shares and the Insurance Act. The democracy of this ccuntry had already given their verdict on Home Rule. They had decided that the people of Ireland bad a right to self-government. (Applause.) Even to-day, there was only one House of Parliament when the Tories were in power, but two when the Liberals were in office. The three years' delay of contentions measures must he swept away— (applause)—for they could not afford to wait that long I tor ,the social reforms that were in prospect. They ' roust have a democratic second Chamber, if they had one at all. (Applause.) TARIFF REFORM UNDER A CLOUD.

Mr. bear Law proposed to raise wages by taxing commodities. (Laughter.) His scheme was, however, lying under a cloud at present—in some constituencies they dare not bring it out. Whilet others were merely talking about raisin 4 wage, the Liberals had done something practical in that direction, as was evidenced 'by their efforts to check the sweatina evil. Four trades were already subject to the provisions of the Trades Boards Act. A taw months ago women might work at tailoring or box-making under conditions that meant absolute starvation. Women were then working for a penny an hour, but to-day that was an utter impossi- bility, according to law. In the box-making industry women must have a minimum wage of 3d. an hour, and in the tailoring industry 31d. an hour, while men must have at least 6d. an hour in both trades. Two hundred thousand people had been brought under the Act, and 70 per cent, of those were women. Seven other indus- tries would shortly he included, and that would mean the addition of '200,000 to the workers affected. (Applawie )

INSURANCE AND THRIFT. They heard a great deal about the first part of the Insurance Act, but very little about the second section, which dealt with unemployment. It was not simply insurance but thtift, and, if they did not require unem- ployment benefit, they could, at the age of sixty, draw out every penny they had paid into the fund, together with 24 per cent. interest—a nice little nest egg. (Applause.) The money could also be withdrawn in care of emigration, and, in the event of death, a person nominated by the insured contributor could receive it.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====

THE GOVERNMENT AND THE SAILORS.

Mr. Ternent tcok occasion to contradict a statement, ‘lhich, he said, was rnade'Zat a Warrington meeting 3 the previous evening, that Mr. Lloyd George had done something to the detriment of the sailors. Mr. Lloyd George was incapable of doing anything against the sea-faring class or any other section of the workers. It was perfectly true that in 1906, while Mr. Lloyd George was at the Board of Trade, an Act came into force for the alteration of the load lines of vessels. But that was not Mr. Lloyd George's Act; it was an Act that had alresdy gone through—passed by the Tory Govern- ment. lie did not know the merits of that Act, but be knew that during the time of the Liberal Government there had be( n a great decrease in the loss of life at sea, and the condition of the sea-faring man, his food and surroundings, had been vastly improved. The speaker recalled the fact that when Mr. Plimsoll was pressing forward his great reform which had meant so much for the safety of seamen, the Tories opposed him on the ground that it would ruin the shipping industry. The Tories had always manifested a greater concern for material things than for humanity, but that could not be said of Mr. Lloyd George, who had at heart the welfare of the people. (Applause). Votes of thanks, proposed hy Mr. .1. Cotter ill, seconded by Mr, W. Miles, were heartily carried.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS MEDICAL OFFICER.

Dr. Joseph Cates, of London, was on Saturday appointed Medical Officer of Health for St. Helens in succession to Dr. J. J. Buchan, who has been appointed to a similar position at Bra Mord. The salary is £550, rising to £650 per annum.

ROYAL HOLIDAYS AT ST. HELENS. The St. Helens education authority considered, on Wednesday, the request of the King that an extension of holiday be granted to the school children of the county, and decided to grant the elementary schools two extra days' holiday in October, and the secondary schools three cla3s extra.

ROBBED WIIILE SEEING THE KING. At St. Helens, on Satqrday, Francis Farnan was charged with breaking into 3, Bolton-street and stealing £2 10s. in money and a pair of clogs. Mrs. Whittle said she and her husband went to see the King and Queen, leaving the house secure. When they returned home they found the back door open, and part of the window lying in the yard. The prisoner was suspected, but he disappeared. On Friday evening he rave hiniself lip to the police, saying that he taken the money rind spent it, and had left the clogs in a house in Blackburn. He told the magistrates he had been drinking tor some time and did not know what he was doing.—ltie Clerk : How did you find out there vi as a lot of money in this house? Prisoner : Well, 1 heard there was book-making going on tbere.—The Chairman urged hint to turn over a new leaf, and find him £2, or one month's imprisonment.

INSURANCE AGENT SUED., At St Helens, on Monday, Daniel Swift, agent for the Scottish Legal Life Assurance Company, was sued for .£3 15s. said to be due on a policy on the life of s. man named Gillis. Mr. Golly represented the com- pany. Mr. W. Webster, for Mrs. Goodier, the plaintiff, of Oldfield-street, said that on November 4. 1912, a policy was taken out on the life of Mrs. Goodier's brother,the arrangement being that 2d. per week should be paid, and that after the policy had been paid up for one year £6 126. should be due. When the policy was thirteen weeks in arrears it could be lapsed. At the time this policy was taken out Mrs. Goodier had policies on the life of her husband and children as men. Some of the policies went into arrears, but hack pay- ments were made. The policies on the children were lapsed, and he (Mr. Webster) alleged that some of the payments made for arrears had not been credited to Gillis's policy. Gillis died in May last, and as more than fourteen payments had been made they claimed half-benefit.—Mr. Golly urged that the policy bad lapsed owing to arrears ; but Mrs. Goodier denied receiving tile lapsed notices.—The Bench decided in favour of Mrs. Goodier, awarding her £3 6s. and £2 18s. 60. costs.

BARRIER TO REFORM.

Wanted: A Sober, Thinking Democracy.

"THE TRADE" AND SLUMS.

"Help us to sweep away the drink tree and we shall have a new England," said Mr. T. D. Leslie, cf Wigan, addressing an open-air meeting held near the Farmers' stores, under Vat!, auspices of the Warrington Citizens' Temperance League and U.K.A., on Wednesday evening. Councillor JAMES SHAW presided, and at the outset said they were there to consider a very important question wLich affected them not only in their civic life, bur, imperially, physically, morally, and in every way. He felt proud of the time and labour he had spent for the Calls'', and hoped that the forward inlve of the temperance %others would continue. (A ppl muse.)

===== 0002601_19130719_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
"A DEVIL'S BREW."

Mr. LESLIE said they bad adopted new methods iry temperance work. Doctors and scientista were now at one with them in their advocacy of teetotalism and temperance. They learned from scientists that alcohol was not a food. rho hops which God bad given to strengthen and make us fit had been turned in'f, a devil's brew which robbed men of their character, self- control and self-respect. (Hear, hear.) Doctors told them that alcohol would not prevent disease, but it did everything to encourav3 it. Alcohol was not a food, nor a builder up of the body ; it was a destroyer. Looking at the question from an economic and indus- trial point of view, he did not think workingmen realised 'that a great enemy alcohol was to those who were trying to build up better conditions end enlarge their industries. Continniug, he said that even the most sanguine aniougst them could scarcely hope that with one stroke of the pen they could ab -dish tte drink trade altogether. Alt their offoits, however successful they might be, would be gradual in their eiTeot. They did not anticipate throwing large numbers of people out of work by the restriction or abolition of the trade. But how many people were engaged in the drink trade? In the brewing trade there were fewer than 500,0t0 men engaged, and they talked about unemployment if they restricted or did away with the trade. Last sear tbose 500,000 men produced drink to the value of £161,000,000, which was £322 per man. In the cotton industry it was £B2 per map. 3he question of unemployment in that respect was not a formidable one. if they could divert some of the money now spent in the drink trade into snore u3eful channel?, it would stimulate employment and not teducs it.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
UNPRODUCTIVE EXPENDITURE.

Money spent in the drink trade was not productive, It did not add to the general wealth. It aa merely a turnover of money. There were great prohlems con- fronting the workingmen of the country to-day. Every intelligent workingman was concerned with the great questions of housing, poverty and unemployment. Poverty was caused by drink, whether it were true or not that drink was caused by poverty. All efforts in that direction would be of no avail unless they had a sober, thinking democracy behind them. He rejoiced at the rising power of the workingmen—the greater power they had the more he would be pleased. If only workingmen realised how strong they were, they would soon right the wrongs under which they suffered. There would be no problem of housing. Thus the drink- ing workingmen were pulling down and under- mining the efforts of those men who were striving to get better conditions. VISIONS OF BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON.

Some of them had probably got visions of Warrington with its slums changed into beautiful plots. But the drink trade lay right across the path of progress, and such dreams were far from reslisstion until the bar to progress was swept away. Referring to the drink and its evil influence at election times, be said that in Wigan he bad seen outrageous cases of bribery, but such cases were difficult to prove. The drink trade bought the votcs of the lower class of workingmen, and the totter ruen were let down by those who were bought. Thy wanted the people themselves to abolish the trade. They were not there to force their opinions on people.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL OPTION WANTED.

They were striving to get. local option for the people which would give the public; in any community the right to say whether they would have public-houses in their midst or not. That was a democratic measure to which no one would object, except, the publicans and brewers, who know that local option would be opera- tive, that given the right of local option, the men and voters generally in'certain wards would see to it that public-houses were restricted or done away with altogether. The drink trade said their" trade" was their politics, and he wanted the workingmen to say temperance was their politics. All the pr‘blems would be easier of solution if they were united in their efforts to sweep away the drink traffic. If they did that they would have a better England, for the greatest enemy Christianity had was the " trade." (Applause.) Mr. J. W. BRADLEY related hia experiences. He had had 15 years of drunkenness, but after giving the pledge a trial he was convinced thrkt to be a teetotalle: was the best thing anyone could do. (Applause.)

Backache Kidney Pills

WOMAN SUFFRAGE.

The Low Wages Paid for Fethale

Labour.

"EASIER TO STARVE."

This we, k I should just like to give a few facts about the wages earned by women in the industrial world.

The lowest wages, as everyone knows, earned by industrial workers are earned by women. The average wage of the industrial woman, reckoning her wage for a whole year, and taking into account overtime, short time, good time, and scarcity of work and fines, is between is. and Bs. a wet k (though often nearer is. than Bs.).

The Board of Trade in 1006 gave the average wage of the textile workers at 15s. sd. a week, and in the clothing trades (factories) at 13.4. 6d. These are the best paid trades. _

It is the less skilled workers and home workers who bring down the average wage to such a small sum. Read the evidei ce given on home workers before a Select Committee (1907), e.g. : Birmingham button trades (sewing buttons on to cards)—" Uonsiderable numbers of people are working all the week for 2s. or 35., 4. 3d. was the highe.t weekly wage discover- able:" (l'ags '4O, Section 1298). . . _

Birmingham brush trade—Wages in some cases about 1.4.'0. an hour, out of which some materials have to be found.

Shirt finishing in Scotland —ld , 11d., 1 fld. an hour. (Page 204, section 2118.)

'file question was put- : "Is this (Id. an hour) the lowest remuneration that you know of ?" Miss Irwin : "I have found a case in which a woman was finishing trousers at I,ld. a pair, each pair taking two hours ti finish, and phe Aupplied her own thread. Finally she gave up, saying it was easier to starva without the work.

Seven-pence a dozen is paid in Belfast (where there are nearly 4,000 out-workers connected with the linen trade) for embroidering handkerchiefs, which are sold at Bs. 61. to 9s. 6d. a dozen. With diligence a woman can embroider one dozen a day. Not so the women who ornament tea cloths sold at 15s. and 20s. each ; these cannot be done under two days' persistent labour and the remuneration is Bd. per cloth. (Page 70 of " Women's Industrial News," for Arril, 1911.) These are the wages of the home workers. In some other trades, the home worker will reach the magnifi- cent average of ss. to Gs. per week. It is argued that these women's labour is not really worth more than this miserable amount. But as long as they, as now, never have enough to eat, their labour will remain inefficient. Where wages rise, efficiency rises also.

Beside, is it quite true to say their work is plor, when, for instance, the Ira cloths they have embroidered are worth as much as £1 each?

If anyone takes the trouble to visit home workers they will find they are mostly women who support their young families or some relatives. The anti- suffragists tell us that men require better wages then women, ber,tuse they support families and women do not. People who makes such statements do not know about the lives of our industrial women workers.

But suppose that some of these women support them- selves only, or supplement the family wage, is it right, is it just, that a wo:nan should;be considered as a man s inferior in the wage market, and be supposed to need less to eat, less to live upon ? It is not right, it is not just, it is had for the whole nation—and extremely bad for the future generation. But this point of view, this demand for "economic, justice, will not be enforced until women are men's political equals: i.e., sill they have a voice in the nations affairs and can enforce it themselves. A vote is a voice.

TIIE ENTENTE CORDIALE.

FRENCH AMBASSADOR AND VISIT OF M. POINCARE.

Speaking at a reception at the French Embassy on Monday on the occasion of the French National Fete, M. Cambon, the French Ambassador, after remarking that the success of the visit of M. Poincarts to London had rejoiced all hearts, said : "The entente bet:reen the two countries has become dear to all hearts. It has been said that great thoughts always come from the heart. It is a great thought which animates the rapproachement between the two great liberal Powers of Europe. It is the idea of peace in the times through which we are pa, sing. " Who would have the temerity to dispute the utility of an entente which, without pretending to prevent conflicts, at least permits of their being localised and facilitates their settlement. France and Great Britain have not ceased during the past eight months to work for the restoration of treace. They are not discouraged and they hold to the float conviction .that reason and justice will triumph."

NEW BOOKS.

Czar Ferdinand and His People. By John Macdonald, M.A. London and Edinburgh: T. C: and E. C. Jack. This book could not have been published at a more opportune moment. The struggle of the Mikan - States with Turkey has aroused the attention of the civilised world, and the foremost of those States has been Bulgaria, over which Czar Ferdinand rules. The history of Bulgaria is a deeply interesting one. Centuries ago it was a great power, a dominant nation in south-eastern Europe. But after a long struggle it fell before the Turk and practically ceased to exist. For five centuries or more it was unknown, but though without a vestage of power, subject always to oppression, and often to massacre, the race lived on. Its very misfortunes brought about its revival. The story of its sufferings roused the indignation of Mr. Gladstone, and Europe almost for the first time heard of Bulgaria, when, with impassioned eloquence, he spoke of the Bulgarian atrocities. From that time its history and develop- ment have been marvellous. The great Powers having discovered it formed it into a State and left it to find a prince. It found one in Prince Alexander of Batten- berg. During his short reign Europe was thrilled by the heroism with which he and his Bulgarian peasants beat back the Servian invasion, lie became the idol of his adopted country, but Russian influence com- pelled him to abdicate. Seeking for another prince, the Bulgarians found Prince Ferdinand of Coburg, and he has been ruler of the country for five and twenty years. Not often in the world's history can any offer more romantic but less hopeful have been made than on this occasion. The Bulgarians were a small nation, vassals to the Turks, with other small nations round them, who were envious if not ho.tile and only united with them in hatred to the Turk, and behind them was Russia, their protector, with whom it was necessary to be very careful lost by act or word offence should Fe given. This book gives the ,try of the twenty-five years of: Ferdinand'e rule. Mr. Macdonald has evidently an intense admiration for his hero. We look in vain -for any hint of a flaw in the character of the Czar. Not even in his treatment of Stambuloff, who after all was the real recreator of Bulgaria, will he admit of any fault in Ferdinand, though we venture to think that the verdict of hist ,ry will be that had he so willed he might have saved him from assassination. But, in any case, comparing the Bulgaria of to-day with its condition when he was elected ruler, one must admit that there is much to justify the author's opinion. The progress that has heen made in this period is pro- bably unparalleled in history. An illiterate, scattered people have become a strong nation, the best educated amongst the Balkan races, possessed of fins cities, and of an army which has become a power tr I e reckoned with in Europe. The credit for this must rest in great measure with Czar Ferdinand. lie has not been much in evidence, but during this time he must have been quietly arid unceasingly carrying on his great work. We commend this b rok to those who wish to know something of this kingdom which has a part to play in the future. It bears trace of having been written in haste, it may be a little too paitial in its estimation of Czar Ferdinand, but it contains en immense amount of information of the deepest interest not only concerning a sagacious ruler but also about his gallant people.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
The King to His People.

London: Williams and Norgate. ss. net,

These pages comprise records of the speches and messages of His Majesty George V. as Prince and Sovereign, and cover utterances from the Imperial Tour of 1901 to almost the close of the year 1911. They make a considerable volume of printed matter, and will doubtless prove of interest to many readers. Occupying, as the King must, a position distinct fr,tm party politics, and speaking from a standpoint where his representative capacity involves neutrality on many questions, it follows, of course, that there is not the constructive sugges, ion in ,ihe treatment of public matters which might be forthcoming in a pr rate capacity. Conr ected, too mainly, with official visitations at home and abroad, and with tate and public func- tions, these messages of necessity contain a good deal of sameness in their congratulatory passages. The volume as it stands is of distinct value 'in many ways. It provides some light upon one side of the work of a monarch. The delivery of so marry speeches on a wide variety of subjects, involving in im st cases a personal attendance at the function where the words were uttered, is, in itself, a witness to royal activity. In a footnote to the speech delivered at the opening of Parliament at Melbourne May 9th, 1901, it is recorded that on this occasion the King (then Prince of Wales) sho hands with 4,000 people in one morning. To glance through the subj-cts and the occasion of the delivery of these addresses is to learn the wide diversity of interests in which the King has a share, and to realise the exten- sive travel which has taken him over the Empire and made him intimately acquainted with the people of his realm. It would; however, take a whole column to indicate the varied topics treated. Here are addresses to boys at school who are wisely told that "the hardest service is required from those to whom most has been given " ; here also are words on the social question with a timely insistence On the import- ance of agricultural development, and the contention that national stability " will only remain unshaken while the family life of our race and nation is strong, simple, and pure." The famous" Wake up England" exhortation is printed in these pages, and is worth perusal. It has to be added that the note of mere official utterance is often escaped, and the mark of individuality can be ob,eivod. '1 here is also power of adaptation to the audience addressed, which becomes particularly marked in some of the royal utterance a in distant parts of the Empire, notably in that addressed to the Indian chiefs at Calgary, and there is finally the atmosphere suggestive of a desire for the "brotherhood of nations" and the furtherance of international amity mid peace.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
The Scott Library.

Messrs. Walter Scott have issued as a volume in their "Great Writers" series a very good and critical account of Maurice Maeterlinck. The author, Professor Jethro Bithell, feels the difficulty of writing the life of a living author, especially so retiring and illusive a personality as Maeterlinck, but his volume gives a most readable statement of his work, combining therewith critical esti- mates and comparisons. Those who have read and admired his "Blue Bird,"" Life of the bee," or " Treasure of the humble," will be glad to have this concise and able account of the author. The price is one shilling.

Two other volumes of the Scott Library contain Ne w man's famous "Apologia." Many editions of this famous book have appeared. We have here, however, some- thing which is not readily available, a reprint of the letters and tracts of Charles Kingsley, from which the " Apologia " took its shape. The "Apologia" itself is reprinted from the first edition of 1864. Thus presented the letters and tracts tell their own story and make a vivid piece of reading. The volumes are issued at one shilling each.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. TIIE EDITOR of the " Examiner " invites correspmd- ence on all subjects of public interest. LETTERS should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information, not abuse. LETTERS must in all cave; be accompanied by the name and address of the writer. THE REV. JOHN YONGE IN A NEW ROLE Sir,—l always thought that as a staunch Noncon- formist the Rev. John Yong() would hardly'be the person to solicit either for himself or for anyone else, State patronage of religion. It appears, however, that I was mistaken, for in his letter published in your last issue he complains of the ornts,ion to include" any ministerial presentation to their Majesties " as a "serious slight upon the Christian communities of our town." When, in addition to this, Mr. Yonge supports the Rector, whose personally asserted chief claim to recognition is that he represents " the one and only nue branch of Christ's Holy Catholic Church in this land," 1 must be excused if I express my amaze- ment at the attitude adopted by one whose whole life has been a "protest "against such an unwarrantable assumption of religious superiority. Mr. Yonge is as unfortunate in his facts, as he is in the arguments he deduces from them, and again it is a matter of sincere regret to me,that one wham I respect so highly should have depended upon " current rep)rt " for his information, Instead of going to the fountain head—the Town Hall— where he won!d have found : _ _ _ _ _

First, that the "important det-iil " to which be refers was not " censored by the committee having charge of the arrangements" ; and Secondly, "That a proposal for the inclusion of the Rector, s Nonconformist minister ar.d a Roman Catholic clergyman" was not "brought forward in the committee," WdB not "vetoed," and consequently was not "debated and finally rejected." The truth is that the Mayor did his best to arrange for the presentation of three representative ministers of religion, but his efforts were unsuccessful, and there- fore Mr. Yonge's attitude towards the Reception Com- mittee and the officials is unwarranted and unjust. "THE TRADE" AND EMPLOYMENT.

Sir,— In his letter in your issue of the 28th ult., lir. Peter Taylor makes certain statements which he must have known to be incorrect.

He says" we learn from their own (meaning the drink trade) figui es that, employed in the manufacture and distribution of liquor there aro about 420,000, but of these less than 100,000 are actually engaged in the duction of what costs the buyers £162,797,229." A very slight knowledge of the subject is sufficient to show that the above figures do_ not by any means represent the number of persons to whom the drink trade gives employment. According to Mr. Pratt, to whose work " The Licensed Trade" Mr. Taylor iefers, in addition to 70,000 workers engaged in breweries and distilleries and 485,000 distributors, there are the numerous allied or subsidiary trades which depend to a large extent, in some eases entirely, on the brewing industry for their own success and prosperity. It is a Very modest estimate to put the number of persons employed by the trade at 1,250,000, which is below the general estimate of the trade.

In August, 1904, Mr. G. B. Wilson, secretary of the United Kingdom Alliance, took up this question with the object of disproving the statement made by the trade, and his conclu.sicns as to the number of workers employed are summarised in a well-known work, thus— Makers Distributors 325,912 Dependants 653,331 Agriculturists

Total...

1,226,783

We see, therefore, that the trade gives employment and pays wows to a far larger number of workers than Mr. Taylor wishes your readers to believe. Mr. Taylor must also know aat of the £162,000,000 for which the liquor was sold in 1911, about £38,000,000 went to the State as taxation, the trade being the largest taxpayer and tax collector. The argument, if such it might be called, that "a trade that does not employ proportionately the number of workers it should, when compared with other trades is a sweating trade " is too quixotic to deserve con- sideration. July 7th, 1913

EDWARD TUCKER.
