THE TARIFFS IN THE UNITED STATES.

Sir,—lt was exceedingly kind of Mr. Charles,E. Parker to take the trouble to confirm my statement that Dr. Wilson, the new American President, was not advocating Free Trade but simply a revision or modification of the tariffs. Of curse, Mr. Parker is quite aware that a new President in three or a;x years may come along and again revise the tariff— and it may be on the upward road again. At least that has been the fiscal history of the States for the past forty years. Mr. Parker's simile of the Americans travelling along a tariff 'road was very prettily put, but, at any rate, they appear to enjcy the journey. At all events, they are very loth to leave it, for there are no symptoms of their retiring frouN the beaten track. Mr. Patker says to. get right again they muat retrace their steps along the same road. Surely that would be illogical? It would be an awful and wearisome way round. If a traveller 'is on a wrong road the sooner he gets off it and on the right one, the quicker he will arrive at his journey's end. The fact, of the matter, however, about the American tariff a is that the great political parties of the States, both the Republicans and the Democrats, advocate and believe in them.

Only this week a leading writer in the "Daily News," Mi.. Bunting, who is an American, I believe, says "In the United States the era of Free Trade hes not yet dawned." Many of us at one time imagined that it had dawned here and that we were in possession of it in Britain, but many have wakened up to the fact that it was a vain delusion. In fact we find wherever we look whether abroad tr at home— no such system as Free Trade is in exisiel,ce. In fact, it appears further off than ever. What about Germany? What about Jap.o? What about Canada and the other Colonies? And in additit.n, if we wish to know where the workers are chiefly prosparous we have, according to our own Board of Trade, to find them in the United States and the Colonies. Both the Colonies and the United States so far as the economic conditions of the workers are concerned have left the workers in the mother country far behind, and all the publishing of trade figure returns does not alter the fact. So long as the British manufacturer and worker are open to the world's competition in the home market, as they are at present, so far they must of necessity be handioapped. If the old President, President Lincoln, whom Mr. Parker accuses of "confused thinking," stated he did not know much about tariffs, yet said ho did lindw the effect of tariffs --and it is the effect which matters —Mr. Parker Rill perhaps, forgive me for saying it is possible it is he and not President Lincoln who gets "confused." Lincoln said the effect of tariffs was, "If we buy the goods which are made at home, we get both the goods'and the money." In other words, all the benefits of the labour are retained at home, and this was President Linc3ln's point in his remarks, and which point at the moment Mr. Parker has overlooked or not fathomed.

WHERE WE SCORE,

The position of the German shipbuilding industry relative to that of this country was thus set out in the Berlin " Borsen Courier" of May 2nd, 1912 : "The building of merchant vessels is an inter- national business, and prices must therefore keep pace with those of England. In England coalland materials are cheap, and the cost Of living for the workmen is quite cheap there .- very: much cheaper ;,lian in Germany, since our tariff Was raised in,tbe interests of the Agrarians. We, in Germany, therefore, if we want to build merchant vessels' are compelled to accept prices as made in England. We shall see that, owing to the increasing buTdens of social reform taxation, the entire shipbuilding bu9inesB, especially of merchant vessels, will be drawn to England again. If we had as cheap material in Germany as in England, and if wages were not forced relatively too high by the over-rising prices of food products, then we in Germany coutd centinue to compete with the English shipbuilding trade for the future, or in spine cases even build cheaper ships,"

WHERE PRICES " soAR. "

Cost of Necessaries in Chicago Goes up More Than 50 per cent.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTONIAN S WARNING.

We have, this week, received from a Warrington man in Chicago one of those letters which frequently reach us from Virarringtonians who have settled in far ay.ay homes.

Their fist wlrd is invariably one that breathes sffection for the " good old town" in which they were born or resided awhile; their next is in praise of the stand the Liberal party and the " Examiner " are making in defence of Free Trade to keep out of England the conditions of life to which they themselves are now subjected. This p aticular correspmdent says he feels proud to read in the " Examiner," after an absence of two years, that the Liberal party and Mr. Arthur Crosfield are working hard again, No one was more sorry tlan he when, at the last election, the Brewery won. However, he continues: "So T,;arifT Reform in England is a failure. This is, as you know, a great tariff country. Good wages are given, but the cost of living is higher here than in any c,untry in the world —within one mile of the greatest sto:-...k yards and largest packing plants in the world. Read of the prices of meat while the pickers are keeping the meat I believe, in ice and robbing the poor." The prices which our correspondent asks us to road, to Fee how the poir can be robbed of food in a country where wages are high, but food is 'subject to high tariffs imposed for the protection of interests that are fostered by Trusts, appear in an article in a Chicago newspaper, the title of which, unfortunately, has been cut away.

The article is headed :-- "All food prices soar. In wake of Washington, report comes advance in cost to Chicagoans. Beef up 3 cents a Pound. Bijitchers say wholesalers pay more and blame condition on higher market for cattle." That is a fair kind of preamble. in the article itself we learn the state of affairs in the following direct terms: Chicago felt the effect to-day of another rise in the cost of necessaries. Accompanying en announcement in Chicago of a general advance in the prices of meat came a report from Washington, D. C., showing that every principal article of food, except sugar, repre- senting approximately two-thirds of the expenditure for food of the average family, has jumped in price, in many instances more than 50 per cent. The Washington report makes a comparison in prices by taking those in effect Feb. 15, 1913, Rod the average price for the ten year period, 1890-1899. The c‘naiparisons are mado from investigations conducted by statisticians of the bureau of labour.

In Chicago the housewife to-day was compelled to pay from 111 to 3 cents more per pound for meat than she paid a week ago: The week has seen a jump averaging idol, a hundred pounds in the wholesale price. This has resulted in raising the retail price, in most intances, cents a pound. On the other hand, it was reported that the packers' coolers were filled to overflowing with beef. Sheep and lamb receipts for the week Fhow o I an increase of almost 20,000 head, but these largely were slaughtered and put into storage. According to the comparative figures reported from Washington, sugar a lvanced only 4 per cent, during the period covered, white smoked bacon jumped 111.3 per cent.

Other increases, shown wore: Sirloin steak, 608 per cent. ; round steak, 81 5 ; rib roast, 627 ; pork chops. 89•1 ; smoked hams, 69•1 ; pure lard, 623 ; hens, 66.6 ; wheat, flour, 27•4 ; cornmeal, 56.1 ; strictly fresh eggs, 56 • creamery butter, 63.5 ; while potatoes, 23 6 ; fresh milk, 401. 'rile prices were collected in thirty-nine important industrial cities in which live one-fifth of a.° total number of people in continental United States. _ .

Note that bacon has more than doubled in price, and that increases of between 60 and 70 per cent. are common. We are apt to complain about the rise in prices ; but Free Trade England, it will be seen, is left far behind in this race of " robbing the poor" by Protectionist America.

Borough Police Court.

MONDAY. Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst Bolton and James Evans, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. W. Roberts. W. Furness, S. Wright. As UNCONCERNICD PRISONER.—Henry Darlington, of no fixed abode, was charged on remand with w.mnding Thomas Donnellan, by striking him on the head with a pick shaft. P.C. Gibson said that at 4.50 on Sunday morning, the 6th inst., he saw the prisoner wandering aimlessly about Horsemarket-street. He had a con- versation with him and elicited the fact that be was an ex-convict. He said be had been sleeping out on the railway in company with a stranger. They had a qiiarrel and he struck the other man on the bead with an iron bar. The man bled a great deal and he ( prisoner) did not know, or care, whethsr he was dead or not. Constable Butterworth of Padgate, found Donnellan wandering on the line near Padgate, with his head badly injured. He said he had been "set about" on the railway. Prisoner had recognised Donnellan as the man whom he struck, and when charged he said, "tnat's right"— Übe Chief Constable said that prisoner's state- ment that he did not know or care whether the man was dead or not, was a very callous remark.—The Mayor: He does not look as if he cares much no Prisoner was further ramanded for seven days. It had no front light. Defendant was in charge of it. Witness passed on and as he got about 10 yards further be heard the car start and, on looking round, saw it go into Sankey-street. It hal no rear light. He tried to call defendant's attention to the omission, but was unable to do so, and the car passed on.—Other officers corroborated.—Detective Summers said that when he served the summons, Mr. Beecham remarked that the Chief Constable was very severe on motorists.—Mr. Dean, of Liverpool, who represented the defendant, contended that he had not contravened the regulation as he did all that was possible to keep the lights burning. His accumulators, which had been charged at his father's works at St. Helens, unexpectedly failed ,and be replaced them at the first opportunity, by the kindness of Mr. P. Tomlinsor., who worked at Messrs. Crosfield's. He could not have done wore under the circumstances.— Tne case was dismissed on payment of casts (123. 6d.)

===== 0002601_19130719_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Progress of the Finti.Smoke eampaigl in

Warrington.

THE LEGA'

Proceedings in nrinee tion with the emis- sion of black smoke are generally taken under the second clause of Sub-section 7 of Section 91 of the Public Health Act, 1875, which says in brief :- - Any chimney (not being the chimney of a private owelling-house) sending forth black smoke in such quantity as to be a nuisance shall be deemed to be nuisances liable to be dealt with summarily in manner provided by this Act." Section 334 of the Act provides : " Nothing in this Act shall be construed to extend to mines of different descrip- tions so as to Interfere with or to obstruct the efficient working of the same. nor to the smelting of ores and minerals, nor to the calcining. puddling and rolling of iron and other metals, nor to the conversion of pig iron into wrought iron so as to obstruct or interfere with any of such processes respactivsly.” AVOIDABLE SMOKE MUST BE AVOIDED.

It is this section of the Act which has been construed in Warrington as putting works so engaged outside the control and oversight or the local authority. But it is clear from the wording, and from the legal decisions in test cases that this section does not permit of an unlimited amount of smoke from these processes, but only so far as it is necessary for their proper working. For example, if the provision of another boiler, a taller ohimney, more efficient apparatus, greater intelligence and care in firing, would cure the excessive smoke, then the authorities havel the power, and it is their duty to insist upon these improvements being made, and this has been done again and again—but never in Warrington, and the local authority is in default to this extent. (See Section 92 Public Health Act, 1875.) In the method still usually employed in coal-fired puddling and rolling furnaces, a reducing atmosphere in the furnace is neces- sary at certain stages, and this is attained by use of the damper in checking the air which results in the formation of black smoke. Even with the coal-fired puddling and roll- ing furnace a great deal more smoke than need be is emitted through carelessness. Pro- bably at least fifty per cent. of the smoke from these might be avoided in Warrington by care and forethought in firing and work- ing the furnace. There is some doubt as to the meaning of the term "rolling of iron" in this section. Probably the framers of the Act meant it to refer only to the preparation of a merchant- able wrought iron, and not to any subsequent heating for rolling the finished iron into special articles of commerce. If any process other than the rolling of puddled bars is meant, it is difficult to understand why rolling alone should be mentioned, as the reheating of the merchantable wrought iron for such processes as forging, pressing, etc., is practically.the same as for rolling it. In backward towns it is taken to mean any heat- ing for re-rolling, and also the steam raising for power to drive the rolls. In Warrington is it so construed? A DECIDED CASE AND ITS EFFECTS.

Whatever doubts there may- be about the rolling, power production for driving the rolls is certainly not subject to exemption under any condition. In a recent case brought by the Manchester Corporation against a firm who bought and rolled steel ingots for the manufacture of wire, the magistrate ruled that they came under Section 334 as to the rolling, therefore were entitled to exemption if it could be proved that it was impossible to conduct the process without black smoke. The evidence, however, showed that the prevention of excessive smoke would not interfere with any of the process, and an order to abate, and forbidding recurrence, was made. The defendants appealed to Quarter Sessions on the facts as decided by the City Stipendiary. The Recorder of Manchester dismissed the appeal, and, in giving his decision, held in opposition to the opinion of the lower court, that the rolling carried on by the defendants was not the rolling meant in Section 334. Thus the judgment on appeal confirmed the decision of the lower court, and made it stronger. Through the action of the Manchester Corporation, the improvements affected in the smoke from these particular works has been very great, and they have had a salutary effect on other firms reheating iron and steel. Gaseous fuel without black smoke is now largely used: Improvements in the con- struction of the coal fires, furnaces, especially in regard to the combustion chamber, can reduce smoke, if not to the extent that gaseous fuel does, at any rate to a very small quantity, certainly to the prevention of dense black smoke..

===== 0002601_19130719_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGER OF BRIGHTNE33 AND CHEERFULNESS.

[ro TILE EDITOR OF THE " EXtMINER."] Sir, —The " Examiner " and its correspondeuts have got smoke on the brain, and it has so bef igged them that they cannot see the wood for the trees, or the benefits to Warrington of a tun )ke-laden atmosphere. The advantages only need to be mentioned to be recognised by all right-thinking people. The greatest advantage is economic, that of pro- viding work. T. illustrate let us take the question of collars. It is probable that the bulk of the inhabitants of Warrington dirty one collar more in the week than they would if there were no smoke. This provides work for a considerable number of washerwomen, frequently widows, who are also sometimes orphans. It would be another injustice to women if the ideas of the smoke faddists were carried out. In addition to the extra work provided, the life of the collar is much lessened by washing. No doubt if statistics could be collected it would be found that the Mortality of collars in a smoky town is three times that in the country. .Collare are represeatativee of a large number of other things such as curtains, tablecloths, clothes, and the like. Conceive in the aggregate the amount of work made by getting these things dirtied and worn cut quickly. We should never overlook the necessity of making work to keep our people employed. Leaving the economic question and turning to that of health, it is very necessary that we should have a smoky town. as the Mayor has told us, for killing microbes. The injurious effect on individuals, not microbes, should be ignored, we must have sacrific- somewhere. If the anti-smoke crusade were success full we might soon have a vast company of fat and well-favoured microbes disputing the passage of the new Warringtca Bridge and dancing jigs on the parapet. There is a further danger of our manufacturing firms taking their works, lock, stock, and barrel, to other localities that know how to value the advantages of a smoky atmosphere. It will be a sorry conclusion of the misdirected efforts of the smoke-faddists if (as the far-seeing Chairman of our• Health Committee hints) Bevrsey is turned into a cabbage patch, White- cross into a gtrden of roses, and Walker's Brewery land planted with leeks and tomatoee. Then there is the :esthetic point of view. We should at eventide lose that artistic stimulus given by the Warrington chimneys when, with their smoke clouds, they curtain the bedchamber of the setting sun. One great danger of the age is of our people becom- ing too light-hearted if their surroundings are made bright and cheerful. During the last fewlweeks many of the buildings have been painted afresh, people have been putting new dresses on their houses as well as

===== 0002601_19130719_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
POSITION.

- . _ themselves, and this general brightness whicla present characterises Warrington has its dangers. ait is right enough when the King is coming, but it is not natural or fitting as a normal Warrington condition. It is not necessary that these bright fresh colours should be taken off. All that is needed is that they, should be left alone, the emission of smoke encour- aged, and slowly, but surely, this unseemly brightness will be toned down until it is more in harmony with the general aspects that belong to this serious age and dull locality. July sth, 1913

ANTI-FAD.

THE RIGHT TO POLLUTE THE AIR. [ ro THE EDITOR OF THE " EXAMINER."] Sir, —The inhabitants of Warrington have a just cause far complaint against the manufacturers who do not use available means to consume their smoke, and incidentally against the authorities whose duty it is to protect public interests. Tuere was a tune when individual rights in this country ran wild,when every- one did what was right in his own eyes so long as he did not steal from, or maliciously injure his neighbour. The evils arising from this want of social control came to a head in the ebuses which grew up with the factory system. Children had to work fourteen hours a day and women were little better than beasts of burden. Their health and lives were continually exploited for the benefit of the power and money- holding class. Many believers in what was called individual liberty strongly opposed the Factory Acts; even Liberals like John Bright spoke against them. The 'Factory Acts were the first modern in4talment to a long list of corporate efforts towards industrial amelioration and social improvement. Amongst these may be mentioned the various Sanitary Acts, tneiTruck Act, the Employers' Liability, Smoke Preveetion, and the recent Insurance Act. All these were evidences of the awakening of a social conscience which has been crystalized in laws by which the com- munity safeguards its weaker members. The right to p Alute the air, to lessen the light, to blacken and disfigure the town, to reduce human vitaliq- by the emission of polluting soot and poisonous inflow, is not so injurious to the public well-being as the unregulated factory system by which holocausts of children were done to death, but the difference is only one of degree. The legislation passed So enable the community to safeguard itself against the negligence of manufac- turers is in itself fairly ample, and would have been effective if it had been entrusted to independent administratore. But the weakness at present is that the working of the Acts is largely in the hands of those who are directly or indirectly responsible for the nuisance. It may be admitted that manufacturers are neither better nor worse than the average citizen. It is not safe to leave any class unrestrained licence to do just as it likes. Many manufacturers carelessly turn out unnecessary smoke, the owner of houses carelessly allows his spouts to become choked and forgets to lirnevvaeh his yard walls. But the difference in dealing with these two pleases of nuisance creators is that the householder at once gets notice to abate the nuisance, which if not promptly attended to, a legal aotice follows and com- pulsion is effectively used. The manufactder is allowed to continue the nuisance so many minutes per hour, and, should he exceed this, he is gently remon- strated with. The attitude to the man who getshis spout blocked is, "Get this put right at once, let there be no delay, or you will find yourself in court." The attitude to the manufacturer is, "You know we have given you as an act of grace not required by law at exceptionally long time limit, ten minutes to the hour, in which to pour- out black smoke, please try not to exceed this, because if you do we are likely to have public complaints, arid we may be o'diged to write you again ; but please do not think any the worse of us psrs ,nally for our action, because we have to adopt it much against our inclina- tion." This in spirit, if not in letter, is the way in which the authorities in Warrington, and many similar sized towns, play with their duties under the tnoke Prevention Acts The arnu,ing part about it is that these author ities think they do so well that they are jealous lest the powers they have administered with so slack a hand should be taken away. Speaking of blocked spouts and dirty walls, is not the soot and grit which sattles on the roofs of our houses and gets washed into the spouts by the rain one of the main factors in choking these, and is not the settling of smuts on our walls the chief factor in making these dirty ? The Health Committee, with praiseworthy energy, insist upon the former being cleaned, and the soot-darkened wall covered with lime- wash. How much better to direct their best energy towards controlling and lessening the smoke which is the main cause of the trouble ! What a topsy-turvey comic condition of mind our grave city fathers exhibit. And yet they are all honourable men, whose chief failing is a lack of common sense.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESS

THE WOMAN'S SIDE OF THE QUESTION. [To LIE EDITOR Or TIIE " ENANIINER:I

am glad the "Er mainer " is protesting against smoke. Many a house vife like myself has suffered for years from the smoke and dirt it causes. I suppcse many of us have got s ) accustomed to it that we look upon it, as we do upon the crossness of our children when teething, as a thing we cannot cure and must endure. Is there any hope for us. Mr. Editor ? There is an old Lanc-ashire saying something like this, " Where there's muck there's m iney." But the muck and the money do not go to the same parties. The manufacturer may get the money, we the householders get the muck. But I doubt if the smoke they belch out of their chimneys means tnoeey to the manufac- turer; it seems to me more like waists, and a truer proverb would Os," Where there's smoke there's waste." Anyway, those who make it should born it, and we should not have to fight it in our kitchens, parlours and bedrooms. The general conditiol of the War- rington atmosphere is a disgrace to the men who have the control of our industries, and a grave reflec- tion on our 1, cal authority. I have often thotqght if the wives and daughters of our big manufacturers, who mostly live away from the smoke in houses in pleasant Cheshire gardens, would come for a month and live in our streets and have to fight the smoke it would be diminished very quickly. Our big manufacturers escape from the t wn as soon as their business is done, and I doubt if they realists what hardships they inflict on their poorer neighbours, who are obliged to live almost under the shadow of their chimneys. What handicaps we have to bear ! And the smoke trouble is the worst. It dirties everything, takes away the brightness of walls, curtains, and linen. To keep our houses as we would like means a constant clean- ing, scrubbing and washing. No sooner is this finished than it has to be begtra again, and it is often a heart-breaking and back-breaking job. We know that where steam is required there must be smoke, but why it should be poured out in dense volumes fr m so many of the Warrington chimneys both day and night, and especially at night, is beyond my comprehension. A HOUSEWIFE.

NURSING OF INSURED PERSONS.

At a meeting of the Q teen Victoria Jubilee Institute for Nurses held in London, on Wednesday afternoon, it was reported that the Home of Rest for nurses at Brynmenai has been well patronised during the year. The difficulty in obtaining sufficient nurses to supply the demands for Queen's nurses W.lB stated to be some- what less acute, although the number coming forward is still inadequate. It was decided that a deputation should be organised to the Caancellor of the Exchequer in the autumn to ask for some provision to be made for the nursing of insured persons by the existing nursing associations.

CHESHIRE INSURANCE.

The Great Fight Against Consumption.

The Chairman of the Cheshire Insurance Com- mittee (Mr. Joseph Oswald Brandrith), in an address to the members of that body on Monday, said the number of insured persons in the committee's area was as follows: —Deposit contributors, 2,767 ; members of approved societies, 20',,)50 ; total 209,817. The committee's work with reference to sanatorium benefit has be.en carried out diligently and from a high standpoint. It lass het itself to exercise to the utmost the powers (limited unfoitunately) which the Act has conferred upon it to deal whole-heartedly and effectively with the terrible scourge of tuberculosis. The number of applicants who have receiver' sanatorium benefit is :—Sanatorium treatment, 08; domiciliary treatrnont, 58; sanatorium and domiciliary treatment, 24; hospital treatment, 6. An arrangement has been made with the Cheshire County Nursing Association for the nursing of those insured persons suffering from tuberculosis, who have been recommended for home treatment, the fee to be paid being 63. pr visit, and the arrangement to be in force until the 15th Januhry, 1914.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
PLEA FOR CO-OPERATION".

The conan.ittee, notwidistanding all that has been done up to the present time, feels that help is needed if the work is to be carried out as it should be. The hearty co-operation of local authorities, and adequate finances are necessary to combat the disease, and unless these are forthcoming, much of the good done will be made of non-effeet through re-infection and the continuance of the unsatisfactory housing and sanitary conditions under which many of the insured population live, and by reason of which the disease is carried on and spread. The committee hope that the Cheshire County Council, which stands with the foremost in the country in the administration of progressive measures, will not fail to realise how vitally important is the subject, and make necessary provision to carry out its high obligations to the people of Cheshire. Every sanitary authority in the county also has the power materially to help in the eradication of the disease, and bring nearer the day when its dark shadow shall have ceased to fall across the thresholds of our people. The proposals for the permanent arrangements for the provision of sanatorium benefit are now being carefully considered, and it is to be hoped that any arrangements made will be adequate and satisfactory. In the near future the committee hope to take advan- tage of its powers under Section GO of the Act, and arrangements will he made for the giving of lectures relative to the health of insured persons.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====

INSURED WARRINGTON.

How the Great Health Charter is Being Worked. SOME STRIKING FIGURES.

When the National Insurance Act was about to become law, its opponents predicted all manner of confusion and disaster in the working of this great health charter. After twelve months' experience of the scheme all expectations of that kind have been belied. Employers and workers have united in mak- ing the best use of the measure, and it has become such an important part of the accepted order of things that one can only wonder that the nation was content for so long to deny the toiling masses this splendid security. In Warrington the Act is working very smoothly. The Provisional Insurance Com- mittee has completed a year's work, and the newly-appointed committee will shortly enter upon its duties. The time is therefore opportune for a review of good already accomplished, and, by the courtesy of the clerk of the committee (Mr. Alfred Tilling) we are able to present our readers with some striking and instructive figures. FEW POST OFFICE CONTRIBUTORS.

The number of insured persons in War- rington, as enumerated last week, is 25,653, made up as follows :—Males, 19,450 ; females, 6,203. The total comprises :—Males insured through approved societies, 19,074 ; females 6,154 ; male deposit contributors (through the Post Office), 376 ; females, 49. The deposit contributors numbered 2 per cent. of insured males, and one per cent. of insured females. The number is actually smaller than was anticipated, for, if the proportion of Post Office contributors in Warrington had been in the same ratio as for the country as a whole, the figure would have been 600. Some of the Post Office contributors are persons who, for health, or other reasons, cannot be accepted by the approved societies, , but we believe a large number of them have, through indifference and negligence, allowed themselves to drift into the Post Office ' section. The number of sickness claims received from deposit contributors was 33, and the maternity benefit claims from the same source numbered eight. WHAT THE DOCTORS HAVE RECEIVED.

The total amount paid to the doctors of Warrington for the first three months of the working of the Act was £2,155 3s. 4d., an average of £7lB 7s. 91 per month. Included in that amount was £53 per month for domiciliary fees in respect of the home treat- ment of tuberculosis.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHEMISTS' ACCOUNTS.

The prescriptions issued in the first three months totalled 20,600, averaging 6,867 per month. The average value was 714 d. per prescription, 5.841 prescriptions were under 6d. in value, 13,845 between 6d. and is., and 914 over is. The chemists' accounts for the same period amounted to £590 3s. 2d.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
SANATORIUM TREATMENT.

Thirty-two cases of tuberculosis have been treated in the Sankey Sanatorium, and the amount paid to the Corporation in respect of them during the past six months was £452 3s. 10d., an average of ,E 75 7s. 3d per month. The committee contribute 345. per patient per week to the Corporation. Seven- teen cases of the disease have bc,ten treated at home.

DEATH OF A VETERAN CO-OPERATOR.

One of the pioneers of the Co-operative movement in Warrington passed away yesterday in the person of Mr. John Henry Knowles, of 26, Lovely-lane, who was the first president of the local society. Mr. Knowles, who attained his eightieth birthday a fortnight ago, had been in delicate health for several months, but toe end came s,mewhat suddenly. He was a native of Halifax, but came to Warrington as a youth and entered the wire trade at the works of Messrs. Edelsten. He next went to South Wales to manage an iron and wire works and held that position for fifteen years. Returning to Warrington, he began business on his own account and was one of the first makers of wire mattresses in the country. He invented and worked several successful patents. Afterwards Mr. Knowles became connected with the tube deparrnent of Messrs. Monks Hall and Co. Ltd. which he repre- sented, as principal traveller, until about two years aao Along with the late Mr Frederick Monks, Mr.. Knowles was one of the founders of the Warrington Co-operative Society and was the first to occupy the presidency. The progress of the organization, from very humble beginnings, was always a matter of pride and satisfaction to him. When, in 1910. the Society celebrated its jubilee, Mr. Knowles had the honour of unveiling a commemorative tablet in the Birchall-Ratbbone Memorial Hall, and delivered an interesting speech, full of reminiscences. Mr. Knowles had, for very many years been 'associated with Cairo-street Chapel and, as a young man, came under the influence of Philip Carpenter. Mr. Knowles himself took a delight in helping the younger generation to a fuller realization of the responsibilities and opportunities of life and his work as leader of the young men's class at Cairo-street for several years, will not readily be forgotten. In politics Mr. Knowles was a staunch Radical, but never took a prominent part in party warfare. The funeral will take place at the Warrington Cemetery at 2.30 p.m. on Monday.

THE SUFFRAGETTE.

Intrepid woman, wife or maid, By Law with man unequal made, Your Cause—on Justice stands its base— No senseless clamour can efface. Nor Statesman's nor Archangel's tongue Can stay one hour your mighty throng, Nor Force, nor Sophistry, nor Law Your numbers vast can keep in awe, Nor scorn nor hate of high or low Can turn your well-directed blow. Onward, still on, you boldly move Though friend or foe may disapprove, Your methods gall the wavering state But keep your Cause before the great. • Like anger'd bees you blindly sting, Injustice for injustice fling! And women of most daring turn Pacific means by action spurn, Explosives, hammers, matches ply, Effective weapons none deny, As ready means to ends employ Their foes and critics to annoy. Abusive names distress them not— Our Premier " Traitor " 's not forgot— In arms their prowess is untried, Then why an unknown power.deride ? If criminals red-handed caught, Their "hunger-strike," revolting thought, Unbars the prison's iron gate, And stultifies the power of State! Advancing slowly to their aid, Wise Statesmen look aghast, dismayed! What Statesman, fearing to be just, Will e'er command a Nation's trust? If woman, justly, !by the State Were given her vote with small and great, Would the just Heavens downward come, Blight all our Peace, and wreck our home? Wm. BIRTLES.

Great Salkeld. The above lines were written by the late Mr. William Birtles, whose death we announced in April The author was brother of Councillor Thomas Birtles.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, JULY 19, 1913.

WORLD'S INCREASE OF COTTON MANUFACTURES.

The increase in the n-u-rniTei• or cotton spindles gives an indication of the rapid growth of the manufacture of cotton goods in the world. Spindles, however, are not directly comparable one with another, that is without regard to type, and they do not afford a constant measure of productivity. The number of world's spindles in 1875 was 69 millions; it has more than doubled, and last year, 1912, the number was 141 millions. Our share in this increase is 161/2 millions, and we now have 551/2 millions or 39 per cent, of the total. We have 25 millions more than the United States, and the States have nearly three times as many as Germany, which is third on the list. Our spindles are turning off finer counts and this tendency appears to be becoming more marked each year. No doubt our moist climate is favour- able to fine spinning, but the skill of the manufacturers and operatives is an important factor in enabling us to retain and develop the most valuable end of cotton manufacturing.

THE AMERICAN FARMER AND THE TARIFF.

Tariff sentiment among the progressive American farmers is on the move, and their eyes are opening wide to the tariff swindle. This fact is made abundantly clear by an address of Mr. C. B. Kegley, of the Washing- ton State Grange. Addressing a meeting of farmers on June 3rd, he quotes in strong approbation an editorial from "Farm Stock and House" (U.S.A.) of April 15th, which says : "As a system, Protection is doomed. If we, as farmers, stand for it, we shall lose our share, and the public believing that it has secured relief from the burden of living cost, will stop there, causing what- ever of loss occurs to fall upon the farmer, consequently the business-like course open is for the farmer to fight, not to continue the system, but to smash it! Fight, not to hold his own questionable benefits of tariff on grain and live stock and wool, but to strip the coat of privilege off from the back of every business engaged in supplying the necessaries of life. If we are to have free wheat then give us free iron, free petroleum, free fuel , alcohol, free machinery, free clothing, free lumber! To such a programme as that the American farmer can subscribe. By its adoption he would gain rather than lose. The time to cure the whole diseased system has come, and the proper medicine is the axe:. Mr. Kegley, who is one of the most influential of the farmers in the U.S.A., sums up by saying, "Free trade for one, free trade for all."

OUR APOLOGY.

Last week we -.i517.1-ed a statement saying that the finest pictures and the best report of the Royal Visit would appear in Saturday's "Examiner." Our ponderous contemporary replied by saying, in ponderous fashion, that the "Guardian" Royal Number would be the best. We endeavoured to keep our promise. But if our contemporary was "best," we sincerely apologise to our readers. If.

A RECORD.

We are happy -to say that we had a record sale and that our efforts to deal with a great occasion have been universally appreciated.

THE KING'S MESSAA.

The mes;a:ge. which the King has sent to Lancashire, expressing the satisfaction the reception he and the Queen had during their week's sojourn in Lancashire, will be found in another column and will be read with the keenest interest by all. Nor will it be overlooked that Lord Derby has written specially to the Mayor pointing out that His Majesty's remarks refer to Warrington as well as to the rest of the county; and, add- ing on his own behalf, his thanks fo the Mayor for the courtesy shown and consideration his worship gave to any suggestion made by his Lordship, "for which," concludes the Earl of Derby, "I shall always be profoundly grateful." Dr. Joseph is certainly deserving of the praise that has been bestowed upon him, for he was courtesy itself, as everyone who was at all behind the scenes, knows, during a time of pressure and anxiety such as seldom interferes with the peaceful passage of a Mayoral year. Lord Derby, who had the " Examiner's " illustrated account of the visit brought before his notice, has written us to say that he is "glad to think that the visit was in every way a success." It is, therefore, a satisfaction all round and, to use Lord Derby's own words to the Mayor, the town is "profoundly grateful" to him, for doing all he did to make the meeting of the King and his people a real and memorable one.

MR. ROBERT PIERPOINT REMEMBERED.

Our reference to the remarkable public reception accorded Mr. Robert Pierpoint last week when, in a procession containing certain other notabilities, the principal cheering was reserved for him, has palled forth a remarkably interesting letter from the former Conservative member for the borough. Mr. Pierpoint is fearful lest a generalisatisn as to dates might leave an idea in some minds that he was unfaithful to the late Sir Gilbert Greenall. All who know Mr. Robert Pierpoint know that he and unfaithfulness are the greatest strangers in the world. If a crowning proof of his fidelity were wanting it would have been provided at the last election when, notwithstanding the treatment to which he had been sub- jected, he came upon the Parr Hall platform and, by that one action, saved Mr. Harold Smith • from certain defeat. That appear- ance in the circumstances, circumstances which made men who had cast him off with- out a thought of the long services he had rendered them, tumble over each other to welcome him—genuinely or ungenuinely we will not stop to inquire—in the face of the rank and file of the party assembled in Parr Hall, completed one of the greatest political tragedies on record. But Mr. Pierpoint played his part nobly, and, though he had every reason to resent his treatment in a manner that was ready to hand, he sank self, and Mrs. Pier- point loyally assisted him to do so, and saved the situation for the cause he believed—mis- takenly, as we think—to be the right cause. That incident with what led up to it has never yet, for some reason, been properly recorded. It may be some day. Meanwhile, the memories that have been recalled by our little complimentary note appear in to-day's " Examiner " and will be found full of interest not only to the older but to the younger generation of politicians.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RIGHT TO PURE AIR.

We publish to-day an article giving authoritative facts as to the law as it contirols the emission of black smoke, which those in authority and those who send pollution into our atmosphere will do well closely to study. The question of the moment in Warrington is, Are certain classPs of works exempt from the operation of the smoke prevention legislation? It is claimed that they are but the evidence appears to be on the side of those who say that these exemptions only operate when it has been proved that the owner of an offending chimney has done all he can to reduce his output of smoke to a minimum. If this be so, then law and commonsense for once are in harmony.

CONGRATULATIONS TO MR.CHARLES MADELEY

Congratulations, in which we join, will be extended to Mr. Charles Madeley upon his appointment this week as President for the ensuing year of the Museums Association. The honour is reflected by Mr. Madeley uponl the town for it stamps Warrington Museum' as ranking with the first-class museums ofi the country. The Association, of which Mr. Madeley was one of the founders in 1888, has grown from small beginnings with big ideals —to increase the scientific usefulness and educational value of these institutions—into a very powerful organisation with great influence among the authorities that own and direct museums. Most institutions,from the British and South Kensington Museums downwards, send representatives. Mr. Mad6ley is the founder and the present secretary of the Northern section of the Association, which does good work in the way of deliberating upon questions of Museum administration.

At the Conference, which was held at Hull, another gentleman formerly well-known in Warrington, was to the fore. Mr. E. E. Lowe (the predecessor of Mr. Dunlop and Mr. Deane) who is now at Leicester, showed some beautiful coloured lantern slides, any number of which could be made from one negative.

SECONDARY EDUCATION.

At the prize-giving at the Secondary School this week it was made clear that that institution, in spite of drawbacks of quite an abnormal character, continues to advance in usefulness and to fix, more deeply, its claims to recognition by the people of the borough. This success tends to emphasize the need there is for the provision of further accom- modation in town for the children whose parents are willing to give them instruction beyond the elementary stage. Many candi- dates who have earned the right of entry have had to be turned away this year, and there can be no doubt that more places will have to be found. We note in this connec- tion that a further communication has been received from the governors of the Grammar School respecting their proposed new site, and that the deficiency in the accounts of the Grammar School for the year 1912-13, which the Education Committee have agreed to pay, amounts to .E 194 13541 d., or about £4 a week.

MR. PIERPOINT REMEMBERED

Former Member of the Borough Recalls Some Interesting Facts.

SHIP CANAL AND POLITICS.

[To THE EDITOR OF, THE " EXAMINER. I

Sir,—While offering you my thanks for the very kindly note in your last week's issue concerning my wife and myself, headed "Mr. Robert Pierpoint remembered," I feel that I ought to correct one date : otherwise it would appear that I was not faithful to the late Sir Gilbert Greenall and that 1 supplanted him in 1892. I remember only one letter which appeared under the heading of "Under which King, Bezonian ? " I can give only an approximate date—say about 1883—thirty not twenty years ago. _ _ .

I can-fix this approximate date by the fact that the anonymous writer, after some remarks which might be takeli to mean that I was trying to upset Sir Gilbert Greenall, after his defeat in 1880, wrote to this effect:— What chance has this gentleman (meaning me), considering the course which he thought fit to pursue concerning the Manchester Ship Canal ? " This was a reference to my having given my vote as a ratepayer against the rating of the town for the preliminary expenses of the proposed Ship Canal. I should not have replied to the letter, but that the writer possibly accused me of intriguing against Sir Gilbert Greenall. Had this suggestion, that I was either openly or secretly working against my good friend and leader been true, I should have regarded myself as faithless and dis- honou rable.

There are, I suppose, few who remember these things of so long ago. For some time after the 1880 election Sir Gilbert Greenall made no declaration of his intentions, and, of course, there were surmises and suggestions made by one and another. In the end a deputation, headed by Dr. Mackie (I think), waited upon him, and he consented to come forward again. He had the unanimous support ot the party, Captain Reynolds, Mr. John White, others, and myself. _ _ _

think that I can truly say that for the elections of 1885 and 1886 no one did more platform work than I did in aid of Sir Gilbert's candidature.

Sir Gilbert Greenall had an indefeasible right to stand again if he chose to do so, and, as a matter of course, he had the support of his party from the moment that he announced his decision, and he succeeded in the elec- tions of 1885 and 1886. Towards the end of the 1886 Parliament, being then an old man, he announced his decision not to offer himself for re-election. _ _

_ It was then that I was chosen, with his full approval, to be the candidate to follow him.

I was chairman of the Conservative Association, in succession to Captain Reynnlds, and I am sure that I enjoyed the fullest confidence of old Sir Gilbert Greenail. It was eventually my melancholy duty to pronounce, what I may call, the funeral oration on my old leader to the Conservative Association.

I write these personal particulars lest any- one, being ignorant of the past, should think that I ever betrayed him in any way what- ever.

Perhaps this mention of the Ship Canal may now appear unintelligible to many persons.

The facts are as follows :—lt was proposed that a part of the expenses of the promotion oof the Ship Canal should be fonnd out of the rates of Warrington. To appear at that time to be opposed in any way to the scheme was to take the unpopular part. I had a strong opinion that a commercial undertaking should not be paid for at all out of the rates, and I was strongly opposed to the idea that the railways, e.g., the London and North-Western, one of the chief rate- payers in the town, should have to contri- bute anSr sum large or small towards the pro- motion of a rival business. I was one of the few who voted against the rating. I remember that on that point I replied to the anonymous writer to the following effect :

I regard the possession of a vote as entailing the use of it if the pissessor has an opinion on the ques- tion at issue. I used my vote on the occasion referred to by your correspondent, as I did, for reasons which appeared to me to be good, which reasons I shall be happy to explain to him if he will

call upon me. This I did without any regard to whether my vote pleased either Liberal or Con-

servative. Should the matter ever come forward again I shall then vote exactly as I think right without caring at all whether what I do pleases either Liberal or Conservative. I am, Sir, your obedient servant, ROBERT PIERPOIN T.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
A CONSERVATIVE CLUB REARING SUPPER SEQUEL. --- " Under Which King?"

Since receiving Mr. Pierpoint's letter we have searched the files of the local Press, and have succeeded in discovering many interest- ing references which throw light upon the state of affairs in local political circles. Below we give a selection :

Mrty 31st, 1884. The Liberals seem to have a candidate at last. Have the Conservatives got one ? There seems to be much luke-warmness in the camp, and the new generation are asking if there are any new ideas in the party or whether it is satisfied with the old stock. The Liberal candidate has not much to say for himself, and there is some anxiety to know what line the Conservatives are going to take. I3Jth parties look a little queer. August 20th, 1884

QUERIST.

Representation of Warrington.—As a report has been somewhat extensively circulated to the effect that Mr. Daniel Adamson, the well-known chairman of the Manchester Ship Canal, had been asked to contest this borough at the General Election in the Conservative interest, we are asked to state that there is not the slightest truth in it. And further,that at no Conserva- tive meeting has any gentleman been asked to stand for Warrington, except Sir Gilbert Greenall, who, at a crowded meting two years ago, was unanimously requested to become the Conservative candidate, and accepted the honourable and responsible position.

On October 15th, 1881, the Conservative Club was opened, and upon that interesting occasion Mr. Robert Pierpoint said :

"They had done a good day's work. They had opened their club, and in addition to that they had received a distinct assurance from Sir Gilbert Greens.ll that he intended to stand as the candidate for the borough. (Oboe's) It behoved them all to work well for Sir Gilbert, not because he was Sir Gilbert, but because be was the representative of the Con- servatives of Warrington." Saturday, November 22, 1884.

_ A rearing supper was held at the newly-npened Conservative Club, at which 120 workmen who had been engaged in the erection of the club, were entertained.

Captain lleynolds was in the chair. _ . _.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Athletic Successes.

At the HaNall sports on Saturday, J. Long, of War- rington from the 26 yards mark, won the first prize in the 440 yards fiat race. In the 220 yards race W. liaseldene (Earlestown)with 28 yards start, came third.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Music in the Park.

The Leigue of the Cross band will play selections in the Park to•morrow (I.n.day) afternoon. On Thursday evening Mr. T. J. Down's military band (conducted by Mr. Arthur Down) will occuly the stand.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Daughter to Mr. Richard C. Brooke.

A daughter has been born to Mr. and Mrs. Richard C. Brooke, who ace now living in London. Mr. Brooke, who was married a year ago, i the son and heir of Sir R. M. Brooke, Bart., of Norton Priory.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Forthcoming Marriage.

Next Tuesday at Golborne-street Baptist Church, at ten o'clock, Misi Lily Ogden, sister of the Rev. H. Ogden, who.e housekeeper she has been since he settled in Warrington, will be married to Mr. Tom Longshaw, the church secretary. Water Polo.

The Warrington Swimming Club's water polo team have sustained two reverses this week. On Wednes- day they were beaten by Evertou, away, by six goals to four,and on Thursday St Helens accounted for them at Warrington by six goals to three.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Engagement.

A mlrriage has been arranged betwean Frederick H. Hamilton Smith, 8.A., LL.B., eldest son of Sir Frederick Smith, Bart., and Lady Smith, of Colwyn Bay (formerly of Lymm Hall, Lymm), and Hilda, elder daughter of Mr. John Ross and Mrs. Robs, of Alexandria, Egypt.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Twelve _Months for Attempted Suicide.

At the Liverpool County Quprter Sessions on Tuesday, Peter Scully, (38), a well-known Warrington character, pleaded guilty to attempting to commit suicide by throwing himself into the River Mersey at Walton Arches, and was sent to prison for 12 months. —Mr. H. L. Riley (instructed by the Town Clerk) prosecuted.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
Improving Our Parks.

Application having been made by the Town Council to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £270 and £355 respectively for the laying out of parts of Victoria Park and St. Elphin's Park, Mr. F. H. Tulloch, C.E.. will attend at the Town Hall on Saturday next, at 9.30 a.m., to receive the evidence of any persons interested in the inquiry.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
Esperantists at Lyrom.

On Saturday a party of the local EspPrantists met the Manchester Esperantists at Lymm, where a very enjoyable afternoon and evening were spent. The occasion proved to be a very fitting one for the prac- tical use of Esperanto, and all conversed freely in the .‘ Kara Lingvo." Tea was served in one of the restaurants, where the green standard, bearing the five pointed star, was hoisted.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Sale of George Sheffield Pictures.

At a sale of household effects conducted by Mr. Frank Hickman, by instructions of the executors of the late Mr. William Dutton, at 3, Grappenhall-road, several George Sheffield pictures were sold. They were -" Storming the Castle," which realized four guineas ; landscape, seven guineas; seasc ipe, nine guineas; cathedral, ten guineas; winter scene, thirteen guineas; Dover Cliffs, seven and three-quarter guineas.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
Liberal Fete at Stockton Heatn.

In connection with the Knutsford Division Liberal Association a fete will be held at Kingsley, Bill Cliffe, by permission of Councillor Henry RTherts, to-day (Saturday). Sports and side shows will be among the attractions and Crosfiell's band will play selec- tions. A political meeting will be addressed by Mr. P. M. Oliver (prospective candidate for the Knutsford division) and Mr. J. B. Martindale (prospective candi- date for the Widnes division.)

===== 0002601_19130719_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Rechabltes' Picnic to Pex Hill.

On S4turday the members of "Jonadab" Juvenile Tent and a few of their parents journeyed to Pex Hill and spent a most enjoyable afternoon rambling about and viewing the surrounding districts from the hill-top. One hundred and sixty sat down to tea, kindly prepared by the superintendents' wives. Afterwards the n-iembn-s had guessing competitions arranged according to ace. The special train returned from Farnworth at 8 p.m.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Clerks and Religious Instruction.

At the half-yearly meeting of the Warrington branch of the National Union of Clerks, the following resolution was passed with one dissentient :—" That this Warrington Branch of the National Union of Clerks pledges itself to oppose any policy of the Union which has for its object the abolition of religious education in the schools."

===== 0002601_19130719_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
Latchford Baptist Boys' Brigade.

The members of this flourishing o-ganization gave a concert and demonstration on Thursday evening. The chaplain (Rev. J. S. Chedburn) presided and claimed that the brigade waq promoting the spiritual, moral and physieal health of the boys and was fitting them to be helpful to others. The programme included musicll drill by t.ains of boys under the direction of Mr. Mottram. choruses by the brigade choir, under the leadership of Lieutenant Wilkinson, and songs and recitations by individual members.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Presentation at the Grammar School.

There was a pleasing little ceremony at the Grammar School on Thursday when Mr. F. C. Her zog was presented by the boys with a pair of silver candle- sticks and a silver rose bowl, in recognition of bis completion of twenty-five years service as modern language master at the school. The head boy (Fern) made the presentation and his eulogy of Mr. Herzog's many good qualities and devoted work was warmly seconded by the captain of the school (Mclntyre). Mr. Herzog feelingly returned thanks.

St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co: Staff Picnic The annual staff picnic of the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co. Ltd. took place on Saturday. Over sixty joined in a motor char-a-bane ride from Warrington through Delamere Forest to Chester. Dinner was served at the Hop Pole Hotel, and the party had a trip up the Dee in boats (with a motor launch for the less vigorously disposed). Tea was provided in al fresco fashion on the banks, and after visiting Eccles- ton Ferry, and participating in impromptu donkey races, the return was made by water and road. Mr. C. Ford was in charge of the arrangements, which were admirably carried out, and the picnic tea itself was superintended by Mr. S. H. Lee, whose fertile mind adapted itself to the problems of making bricks without straw that occasionally happen on picnics.

SOME BEAUTIFUL GARDENS OF DIFFERENT TYPES.

MR. P 2

The above is a reproduction of a pond in the old-fashioned country garden surround- ing Penketh Lodge, Penketh, the residence of Mr. C. E. Parker. This pond, like many in the district, was probably originally dug as a pit for getting marl for wheat land .With many tons of stone this pit has been turned into a rockery or artificial rock garden, and the sides planted with a rare variety of rock plants, which have taken kindly to their

Representative Specimens of what can be accomplished in varying conditions.

Photograph by B. Jones. •ODGE.

favourable to their cultivation, and some of the cottage gardens produce blooms which successfully compete and gain distinction. The rose girden at Penketh Lodge is well stocked, ano, being unlimited as to dimen- sions, makes up a delightful picture through- out the season. Ramblers do exceedingly well, and much taste has been shown in pro- viding suitable arches to meet their peculiar requirements.

RKER'S GARDEN, PENKETH

' GARDEN OF MR. W. I CHAIRMAN

The garden which we illustrate above may be considered a typical small villa garden, containing as it does, a variety of trees, shrubs and flowering plants, with the welcome addition of a well-kept lawn. At the present time the garden looks exceed- ingly pretty with a wealth of bloom. The bed shown on the left of the picture is devoted to roses, with an edging of Maggie Mott viola and carnations, on the right

'surroundings. These, together with wild flowers, foxgloves and the like, have made a striking feature in the garden, and form an object of interest to all who pass along the road, from which the pond and its surround- ings can be clearly seen. Mrs. Parker, the head gardener, isEeen in the picture. Penketh is famous for its roses, and the rose has a local show all to itself. The sub- soil in the district being chiefly clay, is very

bed is filled with hardy plants,which provide a succession of bloom throughout the entire summer. Here may be seen campanulas in variety, particularly the very fine white moo'rheimei, erigeron speciosus, lychnis chalcedonica, eryngium alpuium (Alpine sea holly), foxgloves, delphinium, helenium pumilium, achilleas, Madonna lilies, giant thrift, galega alba, with pinks and violas in Th, !olour scheme is very effec-

five and the daily development of fresh flowers make€ a bed such as this a continual delight. On the rustic arches may be seen several well-grown climbing roses just coming into flower. They include crimson rambler, Dorothy Perkins, Lady Gaye and Alberic Barbier. We understand that in the spring the garden is also exceedingly gay, t tulips and daffodils being then the striking features.

WELSH DISESTABLISHMENT.

A Strong Majority Apart from the

Irish Vote.

RELIGION NOT ATTACKED.

A meeting promoted by the \Farrington Junior Liberal Association in support of the Welsh Church Bill was held at Bridge Foot last night. The principal speakers were: Mr. F. Rhodes Smith of Central (London) cam- paign coromi ttee, Mr. C. E. Parker, and Mr. Charles Peach (Manchester,. Mr.E.LLovp JosEs,who presided, card the concessi,,n ! of the demand of the Welsh nation for Disestablish- ment was long overdue, but they were thankful to know that a star had arisen and they were about to see the realization of their hopes. He strongly repudiated the allegation of "robbery of God" and said one could scarcely find words to apply to those who thus stigma- tised tha just claim of the Welsh people. Mr. F. RitonEs SMITH pointed out that there had been no swing of the pendulum in \Vales on the question (of Disestablishment, which the nation had consistently demanded ever since it had enjoyed the popular franchise. They had no antagonism to the Church of England, as such, but they di a object to the political connection between Church and State.- If Disestablish- rnent were an attack upon religion, be, for one, would refuse to support it. They, had the statement of the Archbishop of Canterbury that the movement for Welsh Disestablishmeut was supported in England and in Wales by honest, high-minded, Christian men. f.Applause.) Mr. Gladstone in his day, devout Church- man as he was, asked how long could English opinion continue, to deny justice to Wales. It had been repeatedly stated that the Bill would not pass if it were not for the support of the Irish Ronpin Catholics, but as a matter of fact, there was a majority on the third reading in the House of Conimans lust week, or, 42, apart from the Irish vote. Referring to the dis- advantages of Establishment, from the Churchman's point of view, the speaker said that not one word of the Prayer Book could be altered without the sanction and consent of Parliament. There was a great change in the position of the Bishop of to-day as compared with rulers of the Church in bygone days. The Bishop to-day was simply the nominee of the political party in power, whom a subservient Caapter professed that the Holy spirit had inspired them to select. When advo- cates ot Disestablisbment were accused of wishing to tear away the oldest part of the Church of Eneand, they moald point out that the Church bad no right to force itself upon the NVelsh

photograph by E GARDEN OF MR. JAMES HOLT, 37', FAIRFIELD ROAD, STOCKTON HEATH.

Mr. Holt shows clearly what can be done with the least promising material. Up to 1908, when Mr. Holt became tenant of the house, the site upon which his garden now blooms was a yard playground. The garden

is now well set out and contains a choice collection of over thirty varieties of flowers and shrubs. Mr. Holt is a member of the Stockton Heath Garden Society.

A POPULAR LIBRARY.

There is much grumbling in the book world. Publishers and booksellers complain that they are not getting a fair share of the pros- perity of the time; and there is anxious dis- cussion as to the cause of slackness. Mean- while, we note with unfeigned satisfaction the progress of one of the boldest and most deserving of modern experiments, the "Home University of Modern Knowledge," which consists of shilling volumes specially written

on. a comprehensive plan by some of the most eminent living writers on , history, science, and literature. Hitherto, ten volumes have been issued at a time—incidentally, a heavy strain upon the 'attention of the reviewers. The publishers, Messrs. Williams and Nor- gate, now announce that they will publish sets of five volumes at shorter intervals; and we belie ve this will be found a more con- venient arrangement.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COUNTRYSIDE

Habits and Local Roosting Places

of the Heron.

BOTANICAL 'SPORTS" HD FREAKS.

Every snmmer a number of various hinds of botanical sports and freaks are brought to the Mu-eum-. In this way many forms of more than pulsing interest are added to the collections. During the present year these" sports "seem more prolific than usual - Only a few days ago I received a bunch of six roses bought in Warrington market. Each of the flowers had a malformation buried in the centre amongst the petals, consisting of four or five fully formed abortive flower buds all joined together. This phenomenon occurs more frequently than it is reported, and is caused by an overcrowding of the carpels or essential female organs of the flower. Sorrietimes roses are found with another but smaller flower growing on a long stalk from the centre of the bottom flower. Another sport, which occurs commonly amongst the clover family consists of each flower of the usual flower head being converted into a bundle of tiny stalked leaves, in this case the carpels instead of developing into fl mers and fruit in the normal way, produce the three-lobed leaves like an ordinary foliage leaf, only much smaller. The same thing is often met with in other wild plants, the goat's-beard, the greater plaintain, and the ribwort- being amongst the commonest. Quite another form of f,eaking is that known as " fasiation," or more commonly as " fasciculation." This is usually confined to the stems and flowers, but instances among,t fruits occur sometimes. It is equally common amongst wild and cultivated plants, and is well known to gardeners and botanists. This abnormal growth is caused by the fusing together of several stems, due either to the overcrov%ding of seeds under the soil, the young planti joining together in a very young state, or to the fusion of a number of overcrowded buds on the rootstock, pro- ducing a fi at -or strap-like stem. The collection in _ .

the botany gallery of the Museum includes many instances, amongst them being examples of the upright buttercup, wild hyacinth, smooth hawk's-beard, wall- flower, rose, ass, mint, broom, liliuni auratum, holly, yellow loosestrife and asparagus.

Potatoes Growing Above Ground. Plant tissues are very sensitive and plastic, and" assume such unusual shapes under environmentll and climatic influences that it is puzzling to account for some of them. For inbtance, we are so familiar with the good behaviour of our respectable domestic potato, and so accustomed to see the tasty tubers dug up at the roots at harvest time that we get something of a moral shock to find one plant from suburban Grappenhall so far forgetting the good reputation of the species as to dare produce full-grown potatoes above ground—in the exile of the leaves ! Why one particular individual should seek notoriety in this abnormal fashion, while hundreds of its fellous in the same garden, and thousands more in gardens further afield, grow faithfully to type, is rather more than I can tell. A touch of " unearth', " genius like this belongs rather to the department of Mr. "Punch."

Another case of bad behaviour which has come under Museum notice iF that of a common stinging nettle of interfering habits. It was found at Little Leigh, where the seed had germinated in a flaw in a four-inch iron under-ground water-pipe. The long, thin fibrous roots penetrated into the interior of the pipe till at length the flow of water was interrupted and ultimately stopped altogether. The cause waa only discovered when inquiry was made about the stoppage of the water. It was then found that the dense mass of roots had taken entire possession of two feet of the pipe, completely choking it up. The only punishment for cot:duct like this is permanent imprisonment hi a MuAeum cAse.

Herons Plentiful. An inquirer who is interested in birds wishes to know where he herons which fish in our neighbour- ing streams and marl pits ro)st at night. The birds may be seen overheld wending their way in a westerly direction towards Helsby in the evening, sometimes singly, and sometimes in two's and three's. The bird does not usually fly very high, and may easily be known by the slow dehoerate beat of its ample wings. Many of the herons continue to fish in the pits at night as well as by day. The birds are very common round the immediate neighbourhood of War- rington, and may be seen any day standing motionless in the water at the margin of the pitm, especially on the Moore side of the district. Mr. T. A. Coward, in his " Farma of Cheshire," says there are only two heronries or nesting-sites remaining in the county, one, a well-known and very old one, at Eaton Hall, and the other at Tabley. But many single nests have from time to time been reported in suitable situations. Many woods in North Cheshire have a heronry tradition connected with them. The Dark Arc Wond, near Alvanley, is said to have had a heronry within recent years, and a like tradition relates to Aston Wood on the Weaver. It is more than likely that herons roost at night in many of the woods outside the radius of the noise of the towns. In the breeding season they assemble, in family parties like the rooks, at the larger heronries of Eaton and Tabley. Toe heron, under ordinary cirunistances, is a shy and timid and even stupid bird ; but woe betide man or dog that approaches within striking distance of a wounded heron. It will strike out furiously and with unswerving aim with its powerful neck and dagger- like beak. The same bird allows itself to be insulted and persecuted by impudent rascals like Jackdaws. They will even quit their nests and leave the contents to the none too tender mercies of egg-eating birds. Heron and Large Eel.

One of the farm -hands on the banks of the Old Quay Canal told me that he saw the other day a heron dive from a good height straight as a dart into

the canal and rise—again -s-vith an eel two feet long wriggling and squirming in its beak. The bird rose in the air in widening circles till it reached a great height and then fhw away westwards. The eel was pqssibly basking on the surface as J have seen them often doing on the floating pond-weeds, and the passing heron seeing it—for their sight is both searching and keen—dived and took- possession. But herons are rot always 6,h-eaters, as I learned for myself one day on the Weaver at Du.ton Viaduct. Going along one tiftrnoon recently I disturbed a heron feeding in the `• Flashes" under the Viaduct. Being curious to see what the bird had been doing I went over to the spot and found a disembowelled toad and the remains of two frogs. Not only batrachians like frogs, toads and newts are welcome to the heron's table, but f mall mammals like voles, field mice, shrews and rats as well. Professor Newstead in a paper on the Cheshire Heronries, published in ISOO, tells of a friend at Ledsham who had a tame heron that followed him about the gardens like a dog. This bird would swallow five rats at a single meal, always dipping and shaking them in water before swallowing. Two Russian kittens were introduced into the house, and one was swallowed whole by the heron. To the dogs about the house the bird, though so tame, was a terror, always robbing them of food when it got the chance. If the animals protested the bird could more than hold its own with its powerful beak.

G. A. DUNLOP.

A New Three-quarter Signs on for Warrington.

The Warrington Football Club has made an important capture, Howard Davis, the well-known centre three quarter of the Coventry club, having signed on for the coming season on Wednesday.
