SECONDARY SCHOOL. -- Continued Progress Makes More Accommodation Necessary. THE SHORTAGE OF TEACHERS.

"Prize Day" is the red letter event of the year in connection with the Warrington Municipal Secondary School, and there was the usual happy gathering of scholars and parents of that flourishing school in the Gymnasium on Thursday afternoon, when Professor E T. Campagnac, of Liverpool University, distributed the awards gained during the past twelve month.. The proceedings were agreeably varied, for in addition to the speech-making—which was more than usually interesting—there were some excellent musical 'and dramatic items by the scholars. Mr. A. J. Jolley (deputy-chairman of the Education Committee), who presided, forecasted 'an early enlargement of the school, or the erection of a new building, the demands upon the existing accommodation having become so great that many would-be pupils have to be denied admission. The headmaster (Mr. E. S. A. Robson) received a very cordial welcome on his return to the school after a serious illness. In his report he referred to the increasingly urgent question of the shortage of candidates for the teaching profession, and stated that the remedy was to be found in" better pay for the ,oished article." Professor Campagnac spoke leutl.7 at.d thoughtfully on the purpose of the school, and made an interesting reference to the iorthy part which Warrington has played in the educational world from the seventeenth century, when the Old Academy was in the zenith of fame. Among those prt sent on the platform were: Alder- man A Bennett, Councillors H. Roberts (chairman of the Education Committee) and J. T. Locker, Messrs. C. E. Parker, E. Plinston, and J. Jarman.

elog

===== 0002601_19130719_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRYING PERIOD.

The CHAIRMAN said that during recent months the work of the school had unfortunately been hampered by circumstances over which they had no control, and which had caused deep regret to the governors, scholars and staff. Sickness had very materially inter- fersd with the work of several members of the staff. Mr. Robson was debarred from taking part in the work of the school for some time by eerions illness. They were all deiighted to see him present that afternoon, looking better for his holiday—(applause)—and they biped that at. the beginning of the winter session he wcu'd be able to take his place amongst them in full health and vigour. (Applause.) The senior mistress, Miss Clarke, had had the sympathy of everyone connected with the school in the severe trial she underwent a few weeks ago, when a surgical operation was found necessary in her case. They were pleased to know that. she, too, was making rapid progress, and there was every reason to beliece that she would be fit to resume work after the holidays. One or two members of the staff were absent from school for short periods. In spite of these drawbacks, however, very good and admirable work had been done, and the governors were deeply indebted to those members of the staff who were not prevented from taking up their duties, for the loyal manner in which they had stood by them and unctertakeu extra work. (Applause.) MORE ACCOMMODAIION NEEDED.

The best evidence of the success of the school was to be found in the steady growth of numbers. For the coming setsion they would only be able to take in sixty new scholars. They might lhave taken in 100 who were fully qualified and anxious for admission, but there was nut accommoda- tion for them. When the buildings were drat used as a secondary school they were denounced as excessive in size by some would-be economists, and those who advocated their erection were called all sorts of unpleasant names. Now, however, the school was altogether too small, and before long either additional buildings would have to be erected there, or a new school provided somewhere else to supply the growing demand for secondary education. It was not for him to foreshadow exactly what the arrangements would "oe, but every friend of education must rejoice in the fact that the school—excellent as it was—was already too smell for the needs of the population, and further accommodation would have to be provided almost immediately. The spirit that ran through the school, not merely in the work, but in the sports, was most healthy, and was bound to show itself in the future in an improved state of affairs in the town generally. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19130719_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
HEADMASTER'S REPORT.

The HEADMASTEEL (Mr. E. S. A. Robson), in present- ing his tenth annual report, statei that the number of pupils in the school tor the present session was 206 (126 girls and 80 boys) comparea with 217 (120 girls and 97 boys) for the previous year. There were 12 student teecners, against 15 last session. lbe governors of the school impressed upon parents the aavisability of allowing their children to stay at. school for at least three years, and over 56 pupils completed this period; 14 pupils attended at least tor four years, and 13 for five years. The Board of Education were putting pressure on the committee to see to the filling up of the 4th year classes in the school, whilst on the other hand parents were resisting the agreement to sign a four-year omtract on behalf of their children, and so some torm of vocational training of a commercial or industrial eiaracter was taught in the school. THE DEMANDS FOR ADMISSION.

The demand for admission to the school grow apace and of the 140 applicants this year only 60 would be admitted at the commencement of next session. In connection with the admission examination, it was satisfactory to note the change whereby the combined oral and written work was lefein the hands of the head- m .ster and staff, with good results. The problem of 03 ass-room accommodation was still waiting solution,and , was to be hoped that the parents of the disappointed candidates for admission would exert their influence to secure an extension of the school premises on a scale worthy of the best traditipns of secondary education. SHORTAGE OF CANDIDATES,' He had again to draw the attention of the governors to the decrease in the number of pupils preparing for the teaching profession,the deficit being equally marked all o ier the c =try. In 1906 the number of bursars and pop .1 teachers totalled 11,000, whilst in 1912 there were only 4,300. The new proposals of the Board of Educa- tion included (1) increase of maintenance grant to a maximum of £l5 ; (2) strengthening and improving the pupil teHcher system in rural areas ; and (3) recognition of sped lsotle nes of supplying teachers in certain areas. It was untortunate that toe report did not suggest the obvious remedy of increase of pay for the finished article, and it. would certainly tie a good suggestion to make the school grant partly payable on the salary list rather than on the unit of attendance. DOMESTIC SUBJECTS. The chief changes in the curriculum of the school daring the past year had been in respect to the domestic work, the girls being taught cookery curing the let nd 2nd years' course, and botany being taken in the 3rd. 4th and 6th years' GOUTS'S. Latin was now taken from the 2nd year onwards, wpilst an innovation had been introduced into the work of the student teachers, whereby special branches of studies were studied in detail and suggestions for private reading encouraged. EXAMINATION HONOURS. In the list of honours for the various examinations, he wished to congratulate E. H. Parsons and A. Greenough in the senior Oxford locals, W. E. Mason and F. Done in the junior Oxford, T. Tole in the 2nd division clerkship of the Civil Service, W. Swaine in the higher alternative examination of the Joint Northern Matriculation, and lastly, the five old pupils who had added to the reputation of the school by University and other honours. (Applause.) As to the athletic side of the school, the results had been satisfactory. The hockey, football, cricket, and tennin clubs; the swimming gala and school sports had all attracted a large number of pupils, and he left the parents to judge of the excellent results produced by a regular course of exercise for their children. Finally, be expressed indebtedness to the School Management Committee and to the Director of Education for assist- ance in carrying on the work of the school ; to the members of the staff, and especially to Mr. Broome for the extra work willingly performed during his (Mr. Robson's) absence through illness, and lastly, to the parents who had given their loyal support to the school (Applause.)

PROFESSOR CAMPAGNAC AND WARRINGTON'S

EDUCATIONAL HISTORY.

Prifessor CAMPAGNAC said Warrington had had a long history in the effort made by people to secure for their children, if not for themselves, the advantages of education. In the seventeenth century, _when the old universities were not open to all who desired to enter them, it was in Warrington that one of the most famous and, most useful academies was established for the pursuit, not only of school instruction, but of more advanced education. At that time, and indeed for a considerable period afterwards, Warrington remained what it was from the beginning—among the first of the academies which did much, outside the universities, to rival them,and to stimulate their activities by pro- ducing work which was quite comparable to the best of what was done within them. EXTENSION OF THE SCHOOL AGE.

In our time, the development of education had shown a different inclination. What had been moat remark- able was the extension of secondary schools, of which Warrington presented a very admirable and worthy example. If a town like Warrington sent children to a school of that kind it meant that Warrington believed that the instruction and training which such a school provided were eminently worth paving, and he hoped that that conviction would deepen and grow. He hoped it would grow in the way in which the headmaster suggested—namely, that the parents of children in this town would nos only send their children to that school, but would keep them there for an increasingly long iperiod. (Hear, hear.) In saying that, he touched upon a question which had been much contravened, and which was open to a great deal of dispute. Some people said, on the one hand, that it would be of obvious and clear advantage to young people if they had an extra year or two at school—that it would pay them. There were other persons who said there was no proof of that, but that what was quite clear was that if boys and girls left school pretty young they began to earn something and to acquire useful experience, that they got their foot upon the ladder and might slowly and steadily advance to positions of emolument and influence. He did not think anybody could prove that prolonged schooling would pay—in fact nobody who cared very deeply for what schooling meant would try io argue that prolonged schooling would pay, because he would see that the matter did not admit of proof. What they could say was that the people who had tried the experiment and had given their children longer schooling in this country, and still more largely in other countries, had on the whole, been people who had made the most of the world for themselves and for those who depended upon them. There was this also to be said—that beyond what could be reckoned in terms of reward and payment, there were other kinds of advantage for which a school like that stood and which they gained from membership of it.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
DAYS OF FREEDOM AND LAXITY.

He did not know what their sentiments might be upon social or political topics, but,whatever their views or parties might be, he thought they would agree that, comparing what was to be seen now viith what existed when the elder people present were boys and girls, there was a great deal more freedom and even laxity. People who were of middle age would say that they had, on the whole, a severe and harder bringing up than their own children bad now. He did not know whether that was altogether a good or a bad thing—no doubt it had advantages and disadvantages. Probably the tendency of our day was to go too far in the other direction and to allow young people, and older people too, whp could take it for themselves, more freedom than they could properly use. Freedom could be properly used when a free man had a law within himself to which he was subject and made himself obey. But freedom could not properly be used until such a law was established in the mind and heart. Therefore, those boys and girls who left school at an early age were taken away from a discipline and control which they very much needed. (Hear, hear.) WISDOAL VIRTUE, MANNERS AND LEARNING. Addressing the scholars, Professor Campagnan impressed upon them the true purpose of education in the cultivation of wisdom, virtue, manners and learning. One of the most wholesome lessons they had to learn at school or elsewhere, as they went through life, was to take contentedly and quietly the lowest place. They began at the bottom and the higher they climbed the more they discovered there were things and people above them. Virtue meant finding their place, keeping it, and doing their duty in it. To be pleasant and agreeable (continued the Professor), though quite natural and easy to a few people, was the result of real effort, and almost painful consideration on the part of others. School knowledge was only valuable and could only he pursued to great and profitable lengths, if it was taken up into the domain of morels and manners. He wanted the scholars to combine all the e!ements of discipline in the development of a character, noble, just and sincere. (Applause.) With reference to the shortage of teachers, the Professor said the proof of our belief in education as a national asset was not complete unless the men and women who had the task of conveying instruction and enforcing discipline upon the youth of the country were amply and adequately recompensed for their labours. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19130719_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMING EXTENSION.

Councillor ROBERTS, in proposing a vote of thank,' to Professor Campagnac, urged the scholars who were completing their school course to carry with them iota the world a feeling of regard for the old school and a determination that, as far as they could. their 1 uture lives should reflect credit upon their teachers and the school. (Applause.) He honed the reputation which the school was rapidly gaining, not simply for the education it gave, but for the character of the children educated there, might be enlarged and strengthened, and that tie time would not be far distant when they must have another school to meet the necessary demands of the population. (Applause.) Mr. PLINSTON seconded. He said there was a great need in Warrington for people to study educational questions much more broadly. The duties of mem- bers of the Education Committee, and particularly of the School Attendance Sub-committee, would thereby be considerably lightened. (Hear, hear.) The vote was warmly approved. DIFFICULTIES OF COMPETITION.

Mr. JARMAN, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Jolley, said some members of the Education Committee realised the difficulty which keeping children at school for several years beyond the customary period fre- quently involved. It meant that if the children were not being trained for the teaching profession they would, in many instances, on going out into the world, find there was no room for them because they were too old. They were trying to get dd of that difficulty by the special juvenile employment committee which bad recently been established, but he appealed to the scholars to do their share by proving that they were better men and women for the additional years they had spent in school ,and that they could compete, on equal terms, with those who had left school at. an earlier age. (Applause.) The HEADMASTER seconded the vote Which WAS heartily passed. THE ENTERTAINMENT.

The school choir, under the conductorship of Mr. Baxter, sang the choruses "The Morn" (Denza), "Blow, soft, Southern wind " (Warner), "Snow " (Elgar), and the school song "Forty years on"' (Bowen.) in admirable style, the attention to details of light sad shade being most effective. Marion Little- wood sweetly sang the solo in "The Bees " (Denza.) Miss Jarman tastefully played the pianoforte accompaniments. A dramatic version of" The Pied Piper of Hamelin " was performed in natural, yet realistic style, by a number of scholars. George Morrell made a particu- larly scornful and vengeful Piper, while Pearl Holman as the chorus, spoke her lines with splendid effect. The other parts were capably sustained by the following : The Mayor of Hamelin, Benjamin Farrar; Lame Child, Muriel Critchley ; Councillors, Dennis Cooper, Vincent Furness, James Wilding; Citizens, Verena Lees, Annie Logan, Lillie Weston, Frank Howliston, Stanley Greenbalgh and James A. Wilkinson. The French play" Le Maitre de Forges" (The Iron- master) was next presented, and all concerned acquitted themselves most creditably, infusing a degree of life into their acting which is not often manifested when plays in a foreign tongue have to be memorised. Gertrude Bell gave an exceptionally sympathetic portrayal of Claire de Beaulieu. The other characters were in the bands of the following : Philippe Derblay, Parsons; Duo de Bligny, Greenough; Moulinet, Farrar; Bachelin, Hill; Baron de Pr6font, Simcock ; Octave, Groake ; Domestique,

It DISTRICT CIEW,

LYMM.

CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.— Last Sunday was a "Scholars' Day" at the Congre- gational Church, the occasion being that of the anniver- sary of the Sunday School. The special preacher was the Rev. B. G. Theobald, of Hyde, whose sermons at' the morning and evening services were of a very helpful character. The afternoon gathering took the form of a children's flower service, when the scholars brought bunches of beautiful flowers and plants, which were afterwards distributed among the sick people of the district. Mr. J. H. Holt presided, and an address was given by the Rev. B. G. Theobald, who said that the meaning of the flowers was that God wanted people to be happy. The way to be happy was to be good, ad it was the boys and girls who did wrong that were dull and miserable. The infants sang the pieces" My Father, hear my prayer," and "Hear the pennies dropping," whilst the other scholars rendered such hymns as" All things bright and beautiful," "Oh see the sky so blue and bright,"" Here a little, there a little," and "The flowers appeared on the earth," the latter item being repeated by request at the evening service. There were good congregations at the services, and the collections were in aid of the school fund. CHOIR EXCURSION.—The annual excursion of the Primitive Methodist choir took place on Wednesday when the members spent a day that was at once health- giving, and strikingly enjoyable. The party journeyed by train to Colwyn Bay, and after devoting a short time to that delightful resort, proceeded by electric car to Llandudno, passing through Rhos-on-See, giving the company charming views of the so-called " Naples of the North " and its justly famous bay as the car crossed over the Little Orme. An appetising dinner was provided by Mrs. E. Davies of Mostyn-street, and the visitors gave unmistakable evidence of their appreciation of her efforts on their behalf. The many and varied attractions of a place like Llandudno cannot be exhausted in one afternoon, but the excursionists did their best, sails to the Menai Straits, bathing, visits to the summit of the Great Orme, the wonderful scenery of the Marine Drive, and the Happy Valley, now gay with its beds of glorious roses, etc., all contributing their part to the day's enjoyment. All were ready for the good tea provided by Mrs. Davies. Owing to the kindness of the London and North-Western railway company in placing reserved compartments at the disposal of the party, both the outward aria return journeys were made under most comfortable conditions, songs, games, recitations, etc., making the time of travel all too short. It is worthy of note that this choir appears to be under the special protection of the " weather clerk," having an unbroken record of flue days for its annual outing, last Wednesday forming no exception to this most haLisfac- tory rule.

LYMM PETTY SESSIONS.

WEDNESDAY.

Before Colonel Groves (in the chair), Messrs. A. W. Calienaer, J. Holden and S. Davies.

OLD MAN WHO WOULD NOT LEAVE His HOME.— An application for an ejectment order was made by Mr. T. S. bteel (Warrington) on behalf of Miss Mary Tinsley, The Elms, Penketb, against William Dyson, Camsley-brow, Statham. Mr. Steel stated that. Miss Tinsley was sorry to have to make the application, but the circumstances compelled her to do so. Mr. Dyson, who was 90 years of age, had been the tenant of the cottage for some con- siderable time, and his son at Bolton had been paying the rent. The letter had endeavoured to persuade his father to go and live with him at Bolton or go into the workhouse. The old man lived by himself, and every- one considered it was unsafe for him to do so. Th. premises were getting into a filthy condition ; the old man kept poultry about, he was afraid to go upstairs to bed and slept downstairs. The tenants of the other cottages were complaining about his filthy habits and the dirty state of the premises. They had threatened to give their notices in if the old man did not go. The rent was £8 per year and the man was £36 in arrear. Miss Tinsley bad shown every con- sideration, but there did not seem much chance of her getting the money. It would also cost her a consider- able sum to renovate the property. Notice of the application had been served on the MD. it was not only in the interegts of Miss Tinsley,but in the interests of the old man himself that that application was made. It would give the sou a better chance to try and get him away.—The agent for Miss Tinsley said that Dyson had resisted all peaceable efforts to induce him to teave. The sanitary authorities had notified the owner to abate the nuisance which was caused by the old man's habits. —The application was granted. MooRE WESLEYAN CHURCIL—On Sunday the services in connection with the above place of worship were conducted by Mr. Warburton in the morning and Mr. J. Upon in the evening. The evening service was held on the lawn at 'The Willows," the residence of Miss Dutton, when contributions of flowers were brought by the large congregation who assembled. Mr. Upson preached from Matthew v. 17. The flowers were sent to the Warrington hospitals on the following day. Collections were taken at both services in aid of the District Nursing Association and, realised £2 15s. 6d.

PENKETH.

CARNATION GROWER'S SUCCESS.—In connection with the Carnation Society's annual show at Stock- port, held in Edgerley Park, we are pleased to report that Mr. Joseph Carrington, of Greystone-road, Penketh, was successful in winning the five guinea silver cup, open to all amateurs in England, for ex blooms ot carnations, any variety. DEPARTURE OF THE LOCAL CONSTAELE.—A familiar figure will be missed in Penketh and district in the person of P.C. Houghton,who left Penksth on Tuesday to take up duty at Leyland, near Preston. Mr. Houghton has been at Penketh for 13 years, coming from Moss Sides. Manchester,where lie resided for four year.. He joined the Lancashire County Constabulary at Preston in 1596. During his residence in Penketh he has obtained a good conduct badge, and also a merit badee in recognition of smart work in capturing a fowl stealer in the early hours of the morning.

PENKETH ROSE AND SWEET PEA SOCIETY.

Fifth Annual Exhibition.

This interesting amateur show in connection with the above society took place on Saturday. Owing to its popularity and increasing developments, the con.- mittee wisely decided to have the exhibits staged in a large marquee instead of indoors as previously. The experiment gave considerable satisfaction. Mr. Walter Kelsall very kindly consented to the marquee being erected in his croft adjoining Bank House Farm. The exhibitors are to be congratulated on the excel- lences of their blooms, considering the uafavourable weather experienced,,nd the competition was very keen. Exhibits (not for competition) were kindly sent by Mrs. P. Davies. Misses J. B. Aitken, J. G. Withenshaw, and A. E. Boardman. _ _ .

PENKETH PARISH COUNCIL.

A meeting of the Parish Council was held on Tuesday evening in the Council Schools, Stocks-lane. There were present :—Dr. Murray (chairman), Mr. J. G. Withinsnaw (vice-chairman), iss Tinsley, :Messrs. W. Davies, R. Routledge, 0. Woodward and E. Foster (clerk to the council).

===== 0002601_19130719_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR SPEED LIMIT.

In reply to a letter addressed by the Clerk to the Motor Union, respecting the 10 miles limit through tha village, a letter was read referring the Council to the County Surveyor. Mr. Withinshaw said serious complaints were made of motor cyclists racing to the danger of the inhabi- tants in the evening, and especially on Sundays. It was the unanimous opinion of the Council that this dangerous practice was a serious nuisance, and if it did not cebse the superintendent of police would have to be communicated with. FIDDLERS' FERRY FOOTPATH.

Mr. Woodward proposed the following resolution : " 'net the Parish Council request the Rural District Couucil to remove the fence ohstiucting the right of way along the Filth at Fiddlers' Ferry, at present closed by Captain Michael Hughes. After some discussion the resolution was defeated.

The following resolution was submitted and carried: "That this Council ask the District Council to let the Parish Council have a report as to the position of affairs and that the clerk of the Enid council be requested to state v,hether, in his opinion, there was any likelihood of an early settlement. RISLEY.

WEDDING PRESENTATIONS'. -In the Independent Methodist schoolroom an "At Home" was given by Mr. and Mrs. John Barton, of Croft, on Mondtsy even- ing, in celebration of their wedding, which took place on June 30th, when they received a Bible from the members of the Church to commemorate the first wedding solemnized in the building. The host and hostess received the guests from C.:10 to 7 o'clock. Games and songs were afterwards enjoyed until 9 o'clock, when supper was served. A pair of bronze horses, suitably inscribed, were presented to the host by the men's Bible class, of which he is a member. Mr J. Houghton made the presentation. Mrs. Higharn, an old scholar, then presented the hostess, on behalf of the ladies' Bible class, of which she is a member, with a a silver-plated jam dish and spoon.

WESLEY GUILD.—The monthly devotional meeting of the Guild was held on Monday evening. The Rev. E. Morgan presided over a good 'attendance. "Come unto Him" (Messiah) was beautifully sung by Miss Mason. An interesting address was delivered by the Rev. W. C. J. Neville, of MitfiAld, the subject being " The invitation of Christ." During his ministry in the Warrington circuit, about ten years ago, Mr. Neville made many friends at Padgate,and upon the conclusion of his address a very hearty vote of thanks was accurded him.

Cnt-ncH MEETING.—A somewhat informal church meeting was held in the Wesle3an schoolroom on Tuesday evening. .Its purpose was to consider the question of ministerial appointments in the Warrington circuit, chiefly in regard to the arrangements for Padgate from let September, DD. The Rev. W. A. Beckett, superintendent of the circuit, presided, though he explained that the meeting was, in the strictest sense of the word, illegal according to Methodist law.— After considerable discussion a unanimous vote was passed in favour of the retention of a resident minister at Padgate.—lt was also decided to hold a special meeting in the near future to consider the details of the arrangement.

GARDEN FETE.

The annual fete in connection with Christ Church was held in the Vicarage grounds on Thursday, -J hough the weather was an thing but brilliant, very little rain fell,and this did not detract Irani the success of the proceedings. Amongst many well-known and enthusiastic church workers, we noted several influ- ential ladies and gentlemen from outside the parish. These included Mrs. and Miss Collingwood and Miss A Smith (Newton-le-Willows), the Rev. Canon F. W. and Mrs. Willis, Revs. H. V. Pigot and A. P. White, and Mrs. Rylands.

In introducing Mrs. Collingwood, the Vicar referred to the ol jects which the proceeds of the fete were intended to assist. He explained in detail a scheme by which (if £l5O could be raised in the parish during the next fifteen months) the value of the living would be increased £27 per annum. Referring to the Woolston mission, the Vicar said that it had been decided to erect an organ which, with the necFssary chamber, would cost RM. Towards this Mr. Gorton had pro- mised .£5O. There were also several other matters requiring financial assistance, and be appealed to those present to co-operate and work for the objects he had named and for the good of the parish generally. The Vicar also announced that Lady Green3ll had forwarded a donation of £lO, and Lord Woltner one of a guinea. In declaring the fete open, Mrs. Collingwood said that she was pleased to assist so enthusiastic a band of workers and wished the effort every possible success. A choice bouquet (the gift of Mr. E. Slater) was gracefully presented to Mrs. Collingwood by Miss Hutchinson.

The various competitions were eetered into with zest and the following is a list of the results :—Golf putt- ing, ladies : 1. Mrs. Smith (Newton-le-Willows) ; 2, Miss Haddock (Warrington) ; 3, Miss Laverock. Bowls, ladies (afternoon) : 1, Mrs. Walsh ; 2, Miss Houghton. Bowls. ladies (evening) : 1, Mrs. Starkey; 2, Miss Haddock. The gentlemen's bowling competi- tion could not be completed, owing to the darkness, and the finals are to be played off this afternoon. STOCKTON HEATH.

GARDEN SOCIETV.—The junior section of the Stock- ton Heath Garden Society held their second garden visit of the season on Saturday, the venue being Walton Lea, the beautiful residence of Mr. Armitage. About 40 children and parents assembled,and, under the leader- ship of 'Mr. White and Mr. Dunlop, spent a very pleasant few hours. At Walton Lea, the party were warmly received by Mr. Wilkinson (head gardener), who conducted them through the gardens, conservatories and hot-houses, which contained an abundance of choice blooms. Two features which greatly impressed the party were the excellent and artistic colour scheme in the conservatory, and the fine condition of the rose garden. There was also an absence of yellow flowers, this being the wish of Mrs. Armitage, and giving a beautifully subdued effect to the colour scheme. The orchard was also visited, and members were delighted with what they saw.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.—Continued. Council at which not a solitary burgess put in an appearance except on the occasion of the election of the Mayor. CO-OPERATIVE AGRICULTURE. " Redcap's " suggestion as to small-holders recalled an illuminating illustration furnished by a district on the borders of the Parlia- mentary division upon which Mr. B. S. Townroe has set covetous eyes. The farmers in that region set up a co-operative concernl of their own, and did all their business through that centre. The saving in expense in feeding stuffs alone, which, of course, were obtained in considerable quantities at whole- sale prices, was enormous. That the local middlemen did not like it goes without say- ing—but that was one of its chief recom- mendations.

" I REMEMBER "

REMINISCENCES AND STORIES OF OLD WARRINGTON.

BY VARIOUS CONTRIBUTORS.

THE ANCIENT AND HONOURABLE SPORT OF COCK-FIGHTING.

Dear A.M., After reading the article in your last issue on " Cock-Fighting in Arpley Meadows," it occurred to me that your readers may like to know a little about a family of famous cock-fighters whom I know pretty well. Of course, what I may write will not come under the heading of " I Remember," but it will he an amplification of what was written last week, and will, perhaps, give a surprise by showing that the sport has not yet died out. Cockspur.

In Warwickshire there is a little hamlet near to Polesworth (a mining village), which bears the name Cockspur. It is not remark- able, perhaps, that it should have such an appropriate name, because there can be no doubt that it gets its name from the sport we are writing about. For three hundred years it has been famous as a centre for the raising of fighting cocks; the public-house is called 'The Cock," and there yet lives here a mem- ber of a most famous cock rearing family, Mr William Gilliver. This family of Gillivers have held a free- hold farm here for nearly three centuries, their fame has run through the sporting sections of the Colonies and the Continent, and the Post Office people are often delivering letters addressed to "Mr. Bill Gilliver, Cock-fighter, England." _ . .

Yes, he is Bill Gilliver all the time, and villagers, I think, would be puzzled if a stranger asked for William Gilliver. Let me tell you a little about the family. Cockspur is a place made up of timbered cottages, which flavour of Shakespeare's period, and has an old-world appearance. And " Bill " belongs to an older generation than ours, for he is 86 years old, and has been taking an active part in Continental cock- fights until quite recently. Behind his cottage is a field surrounded by seventy to a hun- dred fowl pens all inhabited by game cocks of the old English fighting type. From these cages consignments of birds go out regularly. Sportsmen from the Continent come round to choose a bird and take the old veteran's advice, and big quantities go to South Africa to supply the sportsmen there, for it is whispered that cock-fights are held by the hundred there.

Of fights in England the old man can tell many a tale, and he has seen thousands of pounds change hands round the cock-pits. His cottage is adorned with pictures of some of his famous birds, and the visitor may handle the silver spurs with which they tore each other to pieces.

The Largest Stakes on Record. In his youth he was associated not only with a famous cock-rearing father, but with a still more famous great-uncle. This Joseph Gilliver provided birds for the Royal pens of both George 111. and George IV. and regularly attended at the pits at West- minster. An engraving of Joseph Gilliver hangs in the cottage, and it has an inscrip- tion which is so interesting that I will quote it in full : Joseph Gilliver, the subject of the plate, was a native of the small town of Polesworth, in Warwickshire, wher3 he resided during the whole of his life. He was the most celebrated cock- feeder England ever produced, and was remark- able for his strict integrity and honesty, qualities most essential for one of his profession. The first main be ever fought in public was at Coleshill, in Warwickshire, and the last was at Preston, in Lancashire, in 1830. During his life he fed prircipally for Lord Mexborough, Mr. Featherstone, Mr. White, General Yates, Mr. Sitwell, and others too numerous to mention. His chief opponent was Potter, who is the feeder for that veteran sportsman, the Earl of Derby, whose attachment for the sport continues unwearied, and who was seen at the last Liver- pool races enjoying his favourite amusement with ' all the zest of a younger man. The largest sum of money for which Gilliver over fought, and, we believe, the largest sum ever fought for in England, was a main of only seven battles, for 1,000 guineas a battle, and 5,000 guineas the main. This was fought at Lincoln, and won by Gilliver, who was fortunate enough to gain five out of seven battles. • At Lichfield, Chester, Manchester, Newton, Oxford, and Preston he has fought great mains and generally with success. The last few years, of his life were spent in a neat cottage in his native town, which he built himself from the produce of his ability in the profession to which be was devoted, and in which he was unrivalled. He died in 1833, in his 74th year. "Unfortunately."

The cottage mentioned is the one I have visited, which is now occupied by Mr. "Bill" Gilliver. Mr. Bill, cannot equal the record of his great-uncle, but tells of visits to France where he has conducted fights in theatres for large stakes. Perhaps I have written enough upon a, subject which will not give comfort to every- body. I will conclude by a story which Bill tells of a conversation he had with the late King Edward. He was introduced in the ring at Newmarket races. " Tho' I am fond of racing I am fonder of cock-fighting, " said the veteran.

"But, cock-fighting has long ago been suppressed," said the King. "Yes, unfortunately, " answered Bill, "Those were the good old times when my parents used to supply fighting birds for the Royal pits." "Did you, indeed," said the King, who finished off the conversation by a hearty laugh.

WARRINGTON BRIDGES OF THE PAST.

A Legend That Became Associated with the Earl of Derby's Bridge.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFLICT 0

There is a legend woven around the history of the most noted of the several bridges that have spanned the Mersey at Warrington, which is of special interest at this moment, when the present Earl of Derby has played so prominent a part in inducing the King to open the Warrington Bridge that is to serve the purposes of this neigh- bourhood in the future. The legend says— though it is contradicted—that the first Earl of Derby built the first substantial bridge at Warrington to accommodate the passage of another King, and that the owner of the ferry tolls, in his resentment, said such things about his lordship that the latter procured his defamer's murder at Bewsey Hall. Wherever the truth may lie it will be clear from the following facts that people had in those days a much more summary method of dealing with their fellows than now. For instance, bridge builders of to-day have only to run the gauntlet of the wits who use the cars or the sarcasm of certain members of the Council. Formerly a King's warrant was necessary for their protection while at work.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST MENTION OF A BRIDGE.

In 1908, Mr. Chas. Madeley, Director of the Museum, delivered an address before the members of the Literary and Philosophical

===== 0002601_19130719_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
F TESTIMONY.

the young Pretender from going South, and he had to cross at Stockport. Lord Derby's bridge survived for three centuries. The bridge we are now about to lose and replace by a modern one-span structure was opened in 1837 and called Victoria Bridge, in recognition of the fact that Queen Victoria came to the throne that year. The keystone of the old bridge was laid at the end of 1836 with Masonic rites. It has, therefore, survived between seventy and eighty years, but inconvenience rather than a failing con- stitution has proved fatal to its continued existence.

THE LEGEND OF BEWSEY HALL.

The following is quoted from Messrs. Jack's "Lancashire Stories" :

In the ancient days the Butlers of Bewsey Hall were lords of the manor at Warrington. Originally the family name had been Pincerna, but an ancestor acting as butler to Ranulph, Earl of Chester, in 1158, had taken the surname of Butler from his office. The position of butler to so great a nobleman as the Earl of Chester was not a menial office, but was equivalent to that of a Comptroller, or Master, of the Household in a royal household of our own time, and could only

dispatched the father, rushed to the cradle with the intention of killing the child. Nothing less than the destruction of the line of Butler would apparently satisfy the Stanley vengeance for the affront put upon them by Sir John. But the ci adle was empty. here there are two versions of the legend. One tells that whilst the murderers were occupied in their bloody work upon Houlcroft and his unfortunate master, a page passed through the porter's lodge carrying a basket. The wicked porter asks— Now whither away, thou little page, Now whither away so fast ? ' They have slain Sir John,' said the little page, 'And his bead in this wicker cast.' 'And whither goest thou with that grisly head ? ' Cried the grim porter again. 'To Warrington Bridge they bade me run, And set it up amain.' 'There may it hang,' cried that loathly knave, And grin till its teeth be dry ; While every day, with jeer and taunt, Will I mock it till I die ! '

It was not Sir John's head, however, that the page carried in his basket, but Sir John's child, which, this version:says, he conveyed "craftily " to the Priory of the Hermit Friars of St. Augustine near the bridge at Warrington,. where it was recovered by the distracted mother on the following morning. After vainly searching for the child the murderers descended the stairs, and dis- covered the trick played by the page upon the porter, whom they roundly accused of treachery— They counted down the red, rel gJld ; And the porter laughed outright ; Now we have paid thy service well, For thy master's blood this night ; For the master's blood thou hast betrayed, We've paid theo thy desire ; But for thy treachery unto us, Thou bast not had thy hire.'

Taking the porter with them across the moat, they hanged him—" they payed him a great reward, and so coming away with him, they hanged him at a tree in Bewsey Parke." The other version of the legend is the more credible of the two. This tells that a faithful negro servant, hearing the fray in Sir John's bedroom, rushed thither, and entering by a door opposite to that forced by the murderers snatched up the infant from its cradle, and carried it to its nurse in an adjoining chamber. Returning to Sir John's bedroom, the negro held the three murderers at bay in the second doorway, while the nurse effected her escape with the child. Finally he, too, was killed,but in the meantime the child was safe at the Priory by Warrington Bridge. In the second version of the legend the traitorous porter is also hanged, after having been paid the blood money he demanded, but this was doubtless a precautionary.measure on the part of the murderers, who would naturally wish to remove so damning and treacherous a witness to their crime, and the deliberation with which it had been arranged. Lady Butler commenced a prosecution against Sir John's murderers, but the machinery of the law in those days,especially when directed against a member of so power- ful a family as the Stanleys, was not easily set in motion, and before her suit came on for hearing she had married Lord Grey, doubtless as a protection to herself and her child against her late 'husband's enemies. Her second husband, however, did not support her in her efforts to bring Sir John's murderers to justice, and exercising a right then existing he made her suit void. This again, was probably due to Stanley influence. Whatever the reason, his wife did not submit tamely : "for which reason she parted from her husband and came into Lancashire, saying, 'lf my lord will not let me have my will of my husband's enemies, yet shall my body be buried by him.'"

The descendants of Sir John continued to live at Bewsey Hall until 1603, when the estate was sold to the Irelands of Hale Hall. MR. MADELEY AND THE LEGEND.

Mr. Madeley dealing with this legend says : It is said that the bridge was built to spite Sir Thomas Boteler, of Bewsey, but, although there was evidence of a quarrel between the Earl and Sir Thomas, concerning the tolls, it did not arise until after the bridge was built. Lord Stanley had married the King's mother, the Countess of Richmond. He had been the first on the field of Bosworth to proclaim the victor in that battle, King of England, and for this service had been created Earl of Derby and Constable of England. It was very natural, therefore, when the King came to Lathom to visit his mother, that the Earl should have been anxious to make this event coincide with the inauguration of a great public work carried out at his own expense. . . . The Earl paid all the cost of rebuild- ing the bridge and left at his death 800 marks to " maintain " the bridge and to purchase the right of toll. Sir Thomas Boteler, who had not previously appeared in the matter, put in a claim to the bridge tolls, but the courts decided against him, and thus ceased a burdensome impost which, for 300 years, had oppressed the people and hindered the industrial development• of the district. . . . . Legend affirmed that the Earl of Derby invited Sir John Boteler to attend him at Latho-ml during the visit of the King, wearing the Earl's livery, and that Sir John refused. Further, that Sir John, having the ferry, refused to allow the Earl to cross, whereupon the Earl purchased land on each side of the river, and built a bridge on his own ground. Hence arose bad blood between the two, and eventually Lord Derby insti- gated and procured the murder of Sir John Boteler. Whatever might be the facts of the Bewsey tragedy, it had, at any rate, no con- nection with Warrington Bridge. The murder took place probably 60 years before the bridge was built, the victim being the grandfather of Sir T. Boteler, who was living at the time of the King's visit.

===== 0002601_19130719_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
Society,on the subject of Warrington Bridges, in which he fixed the first mention of a Warrington Bridge in 1304. The bridge was again mentioned in 1309, 1321 and 1338, for the King authorised the levying of dues in the market to provide funds for its repair. By 1364 this bridge had disappeared, and a warrant was issued by the King for the pro- tection of the people who were about to erect a new one.

PROTECTION FOR THE BUILDERS 011 THE SECOND BRIDGE. Dr. Kendrick's translation of this warrant, includes the following passages : Know ye that seeing that our faithful and beloved John le Boaller, Geoffrey de Warburton, and Matthew do Itixton piropose to build anew a certain bridge over the river Mersey within their lordship, from a charitable desire, and for the ease and comfort of our people of those parts and elsewhere wishing to cross over at that place, and also to station (or charter) a vessel there called the Christofier, for the carriage of stones, limo, and other things for the building of the said bridge ; as we have been given to understand they are afraid, as well for themselves, the carpenters, builders, masons and other workmen. As for the vessel aforesaid and the mariners belonging thereto, from certain of their enemies and so on.

The presumption was that the Boydells (who then had the ferry tolls) were annoyed at the building of the bridge.

There is no mention Of the material ot which the bridge was composed, though if will be noted that the warrant speaks of the protection of masons, carpenters and builders, and of the carriage of stone and lime. There were early indications of the decay of this second bridge and signs that the maintenance of it was regarded as a charitable duty. In 1430, Sir John le Botiller left by will 20 marks for the repair of the bridge. In 1465, when the Legh rental—the survey of the property of the Legh family in Warrington—was drawn up, certain houses were described as "near the place where the bridge formerly stood." It had, therefore, completely dis- appeared in less than 100 years from its erection. It is not known exactly when it fell, but in 1453 the archbishops had announced indulgencies to all who should contribute "to the building and re-erection of the bridge over the great and rapid water commonly called the Mersey." A forty days' indulgence was offered in 1479,and about the same time the Duchy of Lancaster made a grant of £2O. LORD DERBY'S BRIDGE.

The bridge that is often spoken of as the first bridge, perhaps because of the lapse of time between its erection and the collapse of its predecessor—fixed by Mr. Madeley at more than 30 years—perhaps because it was so substantial as to lift it into the category of real bridges, was erected in 1495 by the Earl of Derby. The history of this bridge is by no means clear, and our earliest account was published nearly a century after the event by Bishop Stanley of Sodar and Man, which fortunately—though in poor rhyme— describes the transition from the ferry con- ditions to the bridge conditions. This priceless verse says :

At Warrington was kept a common ferry, Which polled the King's people unreasonable. None might go to and fro, a horseback and foot But pay as they passed, there was no other boat. The good Earl considering the people's cost Being tediona to pass by boat or by barge; The Earl made a goodly bridge on his own cost and charge With another good and substantial purveyance. This was a noble heart, liberal and kind -

The people will pray for him time out of mind. The bridge had a stirring history. James I. crossed it to Bewsey on the memorable progress in 1617, when he knighted the loin of beef, Sirloin. During the Civil War the bridge was" The greatest key to Lancashire." It witnessed many important incidents, and an Earl Derby passed over his predecessor's gift to his execution at Bolton. Its two middle arches were taken down to prevent

===== 0002601_19130719_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
be held by a knight of good family. In later generations the descendant of the first Butler married the heiress of Matthew Villiers, Lord of Warrington, and so became possessed of Bewsey Hall and its surrounding property. _ _ _

Amongst the many rights appertaining to the lord of the manor of Warrington was that of a ferry across the Mersey, which was the only communication between the Lanca- shire and Cheshire sides, a monopoly which brought a goodly number of groats each year to the Butler coffers. This monopoly con- tinued until the reign of Henry VII. (1485), and was the actual cause which led to Sir John Butler's murder. Henry VII., being about to pay his historic visit to his step- father, the first Earl of Derby, at Lathom House, it was found that the royal party would have to cross the Mersey by Sir John Butler's ferry, a passage that would not only cause many hours' delay, but which was not unaccompanied by danger, seeing the large number of horses and heavily laden sumpter mules. The Earl of Derby, therefore, being desirous of removing any possible inconven- ience from the journey of his royal stepson, conceived the idea of building a bridge. He owned the land on the Cheshire side of the river, but was compelled to buy a piece of ' ground, belonging to one Norris, of Warring- ton, on the Lancashire side. Immediately the purchase was effected he " builded a bridge at Warrington on both sides, being his own land," to the great disgust and annoyance of Sir John Butler, whose ferry was now rendered useless, and he himself deprived of the good income it had produced. Sir John expressed his opinion of the Earl's action in building the bridge in no measured terms,and consequently a fierce quarrel broke out between the two. But when the King was approaching Warrington the Earl, apparently anxious to bring the quarrel to an end, sent a request to Sir John Butler that he should make one of the train of Lanca- shire noblemen and gentlemen who were accompanying him to meet the monarch. Sir John sent a contemptuous and most discourteous refusal.

The Earl of Derby—by reason of his relationship by marriage to Henry VII., and his vast possessions—was then all para- mount in Lancashire, and such an affront to his high dignity could not be passed lightly by. Whether the Earl himself had any part ' in the dastardly means taken to avenge the insult is not known, but the name of his son, Lord Stanley, is given by the old chronicler as the instigator of the crime. "Sir John Butler, knt., was slaine in his bedde by the procurement of the Lord Standley, Sir Piers Leigh and Mr. William Savage joining with him in that action." The legend runs that, having bribed the porter at Bewsey Hall, the latter, in the dead of the night, set a light in one of the windows to serve as a guide to the murderers across the broad moat, which they crossed in a "coracle," or leather boat. What hideous thing c3mes swift and dark Athwart that flickering wave? A spectre boat there seems to glide, With many an uplift glaive. The bolts are unslid by that grim porter, And a gladsome man was he, When three foemen fierce strode up the stair All trim and cautiously.

The traitorous porter led the three murderers to Sir John's sleeping chamber, but at the door they found his chamberlain, Houlcroft, guarding his master's slumbers. In vain they parleyed with him, but the faithful servant would not, give them admission, and, after a desperate resistance, was hewn to the ground. The noise of the combat, the clashing of swords and the cries of the devoted Houlcroft had awakened Sir John, who, leaping from his bed, was fallen upon and hacked to pieces by the murderers before he could defend himself.

Lady Butler was sleeping in the same room together with Sir John's infant son and heir. The miscreants spared her life, but, having

NEW FIBRES FOR TEXTILE USE.

Fabrics known by the general name of vegetable silk are now being made from a number of vegetable fibres, and owing to recent researches this number' is likely to in- crease before long. The fibre known as kapok has been in use for some time as a stuffing material for life-belts, mattresses, and cushions, and it is now coming into use for making vegetable silk. At Chemnitz, Germany, the manufacture has been going on for several years past, and the kapok fibres are first put through a elyzmical treat- ment before spinning into threads. The kapok fibre comes from the plant known as Ceiba pentandra, but it is not the only source of vegetable silk, for in fact the same is making use of the " (Own " fibre, this being' taken from the plant. Calotropis procera and the like, which are of common growth in India, the Malay Archipelago, and East Africa. Good results are also obtained with the fibres of the Chlorocodon Whytei, a vine growing in the region of Amami. Another source of fibre is the material which surrounds the seeds of the kickxia elastiea and others, and by a proper treatment these can be made up into threads which have somewhat the same lustre as silk.

THE MARVEL OF A MONKEY'S TAIL. It is not supposed to be a sign of elevated taste to be found taking an interest in monkeys, and not many full-grown intelligent folks would, I suppose, feel proud to be found standing round a monkey cage. Yet there are features of these tricky creatures in which nobody can fail to be just a little interested, and I propose to mention one of them.

The thing that attracts me most in watch- ing a monkey is not his dexterity of hand or limit, but his curious dexterity of tail. In cage life he has not very much scope for its use beyond perhaps an occasional swing from a perch, but in the forest it is . discovered to be an appendage of extraordinary strength and usefulness.

Of course, tails have not been very evenly given out among the monkey tribes. Apes have none at all,and baboons only short ones, but the real monkey has a yard or two of sinewy material stretched out behind that looks as though it must very often get in the way. It will, perhaps, give you the best idea of the strength of a monkey's tail if I tell you how they are used by the wild monkeys of South America. These creatures are much given to travelling, and in the course of travels through rough forests have often some engineering feats to perform. There are, for instance, broad streams and rivers to be crossed. This is what happens when the stream is too broad to be jumped. Some of the older monkeys will seek out a place where there are trees on each side of the water. One of the strongest monkeys will then wrap his tail roung a high branch and hang. A second will slip down his body and wrap his tail securely around him. Others do the same until there is a line of hanging monkeys, each holding by his tail. A strong one takes the lowest place and sets the string swinging by running along the ground. By giving a push on the ground each time he swings past a good momentum is gained, and after a series of frantic efforts he is able to grasp a branch of the tree on the other side.

A living bridge is now available and the tribe passes over in procession. The bringing over of the bridge monkeys (whose tails must surely be getting tired) is another ingenious bit of engineering. T*To or three athletic monkeys take hold of the end monkey who is clutching by his bands and drag him up higher and higher. They lengthen the bridge and hook themselves up to the highest branch available. Then there is a yelp •which is a signal for monkey number one to loose his tail. He does so, and comes with a heavy splash into the river. But the living chain holds fast and they climb up each others bodies out of the water.

If I were trying to find a good lesson for boys and girls I should point to the forest monkeys as giving a good illustration of what can be done by co-operation and friendliness. But I did not set out to find a moral. It was only to get you interested in the thin end of the animal.

Just think of the strength of that first monkey's tail!

WEEKLY SMILE. A CLEVER RETORT.

In one of the villages of Carnarvonshire, a pompous old squire met one day an old Welsh woman who was returning from the post office with her five shillings old-age pension. The squire knew her opinions. "I suppose that some day Lloyd George will build you a special railway direct to heaven ?" he asked. The old woman looked at him full in the face. "No, sir, not exactly that ; but he is making the waiting-room very comfortable."

A LEOPARD STORY.

Rather a curious incident is related by Mr. F. W. Siedentoff in the Usambara Post, German East Africa. A leopard had become a nuisance by killing the dogs belonging to a chief, as well as several in the possession of Europeans. Since he had no trap and there was no moon, 'Oho writer had determined to shoot it by lantern light, as he knew the leopard would not be afraid of the light if hung high enough. He procured a goat and fagtened it to a bush at the back of his tent and about 14ft. away. The lantern was bung at a height of 9ft., and at about eight yards' distance from the goat. Mr. Siedentoff then went into his tent, fastening back the door sufficieittly to leave space for the barrel of his gun. After about, two hours' wait, during which the goat had stood motionless wiih- out uttering a sound, something suddenly ap- proached with .a good deal of noise, whieh, as he rightly supposed, was the leopard. When the latter noticed that the goat was tied it let it drop and sprang a few feet to the Other side of it, and stood motionles.s with all its senses on the qui vivo. To continue in Mr. Siedentoff's own words: "Although the leopard's attack had taken me by surprise, yet I had already Stuck my gun through the opening. However, as I could not see the sight of the gm", I had the ill-luck to miss with my first shot. Th,

REPORT SOUNDED LIKE THUNDER,

and echoed and re-echoed from the rocky sides of the hill. To my great astonishment, • bow-. ever, the leopard had not decamped but ‘still stood perfecitlj• still, thus giving me tbe oppor- tunity of a second shot at its beautifully-spotted hide, which it acknowledged with a powerful backward spring. _I ran quickly out of the tent, had a blazing fire made of straw, and looked for the robber whose death-rattle . I had heard shortly after the second discharge. I found it lying dead hardly twelve yards from where I shot at it. Did the leopard think the first flash and report mere lightning and thunder? In no other way can I explain idle matter, for it does sometimes happen that lightning and thunder follow one another very closely. Is it possible it did not hear the bullet that struck a stone hardly two yards behind it? Or was it the well-known audacity it is accustomed to develop at night? It is an unquestioned fact that leopards have several times talirn dogs out of Europeans' tents at night."

CURES ALL

The Examiner, Saturday, July 19, 1913.

him in everything, as though he had a right to be first. Very often, during the vacations, Lord Granleigh would join the youth of the village 4n the cricket and the football fields. The _woman wondered why the young Oholonian differed from his fellows; why he • did not show what he must- surely feel—the honour oi playing with him—the 'son of the Marquess of Thorely—and his friends, who also were the sons of noblemen. Jack Martin was too much at his ease in their presence to please Lord Granleigh. He seemed not to see the enormous gulf between them and himself. Not that he was at all vulgar or unduly free in his manner. No, he was always polite; but his demeanour was the same to all alike. What Lord Granleigh and his friends really wanted" from Jack was servility; therefore, not getting it, they took a dislike to him, and were impertinent—often downright insulting --4 o him on every possible occasion. The end of it was that Jack refused to play with Lord Granleigh and his friends, and as his com- rades followed his example, this increased the dislike the young nobleman entertained for him. Could Granleigh have charged Jack with any punishable misdemeanour,' he would gladly have done so. But in the whole county—in all England—there was not a better behaved lad than he; it was not in the power of anyone to say a word against his character.

While Lord Granleigh was still at Eton his father was killed in an automobile accident, ani for a time the family went abroad, and the Hall was closed.

Not until he had reached his majority, and finished his studies at Oxford, did the new Marquess return to Childerditch. Meanwhile, Jack had fallen in love with pretty Nellie Clerkson, and she had promised to be his wife in the following spring. Nelly was undoubtedly the very prettiest girl for miles around; and many a young far- mer patronised her father's inn, just for the pleasure of being served by her. Clerkson had, at one time, been the late Lord Thorely's stud-groom, and quite a num- ber of gentlemen of the neighbourhood con- sulted him on matters of horseflesh. Lord Thorely, too, when he came again to Childer- ditch, had something to say to Clerkson, and called at the Hen and- Chickens. The young nobleman was not behind ,the rest of the mas- culine world in bestowing glances of admira- tion upon the innkeeper's pretty daughter. His admiration, however, was not confined to glances. The tongue did more work than the eye, and he soon persuaded the girl to walk with him in the park. She was very young, and the admiration of my Lord flattered her. She liked to listen to his praises of her beauty and grace; and when he told her that she far surpassed the great ladies of his world, she believed him, and was delighted. Now, you miust not suppose that Nelly did not love Jack; indeed, she was exceedingly. fond of him, and she had felt no small amount of pride when her engagement to him became known in the village. But it was dull at Childerditch, and this flirtation with the gentleman amused her. Of course, she never mentioned Lord Thorely's name to Jack; she knew they disliked each other. She hardly cared to hear Lord Thorely talk against her future husband, as he did very often. His assurance that she, with her great beauty, was far too good for a village black- smith made her uneasy for a little while. However, all doubts of Jack's inferiority to her, and of her strength of love for him, quickly vanished when in his presence. These clandestine meetings had been many. before Jack heard about them. A young gamekeeper informed him that he might see the woman he intended to make his wife almost any afternoon strolling in a certain part of the park with his Lordship. Jack disbelieved the man at first. Nelly— his Nelly, his promised wife—walking in the park with another man, and that mAn Lord Thorely! He thought the keeper had mis- taken someone else for her. Surely Nelly could not be guilty of such conduct? Then he resolved to go himself and see into the truth of it.

Yes, there was his future wife, seated by the side of Lord Thorely on a fallen tree. It was with great difficulty that Jack restrained himself. His impulse was to rush forward and drag Nally Clerkson away from the man's side. What he saw then nearly maddened him. A tress of the girl's hair formed .a play- thing for Lord Thorely. It had escaped from its fastening, and he twisted and retwisted it round his fingers, as he murmured nonsense into her car. Nelly tried to draw the hair from out of his hand, laughing lightly the while; but he only held it more firmly, and, at last, raised it to his lips. Then he threw his arms round her, and tried to kiss her on the mouth. A struggle ensued. Nelly was indignant— more, she ;was frightened, and she gave a little scream.

Jack darted forward from out of a clump of trees, and fiercely seizing hold of his sweet- heart, dragged her away from Lord Thorely. The nobleman raised his cane menacingly.. ..

"Put down your cane, you scoundiel,", Jack cried, "or I will take it out of your hand and use it about your back." "What—what—you dare speak such words to me! "he exclaimed, growing white with rage. "You low-born hound! We'll see what a good lashing will do for you," and he there- upon laid the cane about Jack's body like a madman.

Nelly was terrified; she tried to stay Thorely's arm. Jack thrust her behind himu and with a firm, well-aimed bloW, struck the nobleman full between the eyes. Without a cry or a sound he fell back upon the turf, and the hand relaxed the hold of the cane.

Jack Martin was arrested for assault. Ile must have gone mad for the moment, or per- ' haps he had been drinking, was t-he opinion of the vicar. It seemed such a terrible and unpardonable crime, in his eyes, for a man in Jack Martin's humble position to raise hand against the Marquess of Thorely, no mat- ter what the provocation might be. Of course, the blacksmith deserved severe punishment. But Jack was not punished. He had struck Lord Thorely in self-defence. The case was dismissed.

From this time Lord Thorely bore Jack Martin an undying enmity. Having been un- able to get him punished by the law of his country, he vowed he should suffer by other means. One day a thought struck him, and he immediately sent for his steward. " Coverdale, he began, "I wish Martin, the blacksmith, to be turned out of the cot- tage he occupies. And you must see about finding more respeetable tenants for it and the workshop attached. And, mind, I won't allow him to set up :As show anywhere else on my estate. He and his wife and his black- guard son must clear right away." "Pardon me, my Lord," returned the steward, "you haven't a better tenant in Childerditch than Martin, the blacksmith. Why, he was born in that cottage, also his father; and his grandfather, I have been told, wn,s the village blacksmith in your great- grandfather's time. No one, my Lord, can say a word against poor old Martin."' "You understand my orders, sir—eh? I ex- pect you to execute them without commeNt," and Lord Thorely made a gesture of dismissal. It came as a positive shock to the old black- smith—the notice to quit the home that had been his all the days of his life. It was in- deed cruel of my Lord, he said. He had done nothing to deserve this. "He's tryin' to punish our Jack through us," said his wife. ." But be easy, Martin, We'll not leave this. You was born here, an' you'll die here, if the good Master leaves me my wits a little bit longer. Th' time's come when th' right man'll, be put in th' right place. Ay, this cottage is as good as our very own. Turn out? No indeed!"

When Jack joined them at supper he re- marked a look of restrained excitement on tho faces of his parents. ' "You're getting over the trouble, aren't you, dad?" he said, au he slipped into his place at the table. "We'll soon make a com- fortable home somewhere else, mother. I Ihimk we'd better move into Brentwood town; and I'll do my best to make a business there. A innu was telling me this morning that old Hallet's dead, and his shop's to let. I have not been educated for any- thing else but a blacksmith. I am happy as 1. am. But my children—should God bless me by giving, me such—shall have their rights from the hour of their birth." "We arc brothers," said Lord Osborn, ex- tending his hand. "Can we not be friends also? "

OBJECTORS TO UNION JACKS

Scene at Leigh Council. At. the Leigh ['own Council on Tuesday night, two Socialist councillors moved and seconded an amend- ment that the item of £l5 2s. 4d. for supplying small Union Jacks to 'be eehool children in the borough an account of the King's visit be referred back. Mr. Gregory haid they were to be called upon to pay for halfpenny flags for the children to wave in order to show their loyalty. It placed the Council in a ridiculous rosition. He had nothing to say against the King and Queen; they were quite as good and probably better than most of them, but he was speaking against the principle. Mr. Crooke, another Socialist, said it was absolutely wasting public money to buy these little flags, which were nothing but an expression of bloody warfare. There were cries of" Order "and general uproar,and the Mayor said he would not listen to that language, and ordered Mr. Crooke to sit down. _ _

Mr. Ashworth (Liberal) said the few members of the Council did not represent the opinion of the town. Other councillors also protested arainat the language used, and the Mayor said it be had to ask Councillor Gregory to leave the chamber it would be very awkward.

For the amendment only four voted; there were eighteen against, and it was lost.

SNAKE FASCINATED BY CAT.

, It is staled that the cat has hypnotic powers ever snakes. A gentleman who had lived forty- six years in India told me, says a correspondent in the Field, that his life had been twice saved by his eat. "On one occasion he walked on to the verandah and actually !trod on a cobra that was sitting up ready to strike. Ho expected every moment to feel the deadly fangs, and won- dered why it delayed, when, looking round, he noticed his cat crouching and gazing 'alt the rep- tile steadily with mesmeric effect, for it seemed unable to`wove. and while in this condition he killed it. ANtother time, when sleeping on an open cot in the'verandah, he woke to find on one side of the bed a burr(' cobra with head erect ready to strike, Qui tho ether the glistening green eyes of his cat. The snake remained quite still, so he ran into the room to fetch a gun, and corn- ing back poked the reptile's head with The bar- rels, but it did not take the slightest notice, and seemed like a stuffed and lifeless creature. He then placed his hand in front of the cat's eyes by way of expeviment, when immediately the spell was removed, the cobra raised itself, hissed, and assumed an offensive attitude. He then took his hand away, and the reptile became transfixed again, in which atato it was shot.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS.

The King's Ancestor and his Income from Soap Monopolies.

AN ARTISTIC LITTLE GARMENT.

Not the least interesting of the many manufactures and industries seen in working by the King and Queen, during their recent visit to the County Palatine, was that of soap, a flourishing and lucrative undertaking, to judge from the wealth acquired by this particular trade. Changes have been wrought by time in this as in other indus- tries, and it is interesting to note in connec- tion with King George's visit to a big local factory, that his ancestor James I. derived no less a sum than £30,000 per annum from the monopoly he granted, in the beginning of his reign, to the company of soapmakers at Westminster, who paid him six pounds on every ton they produced. In return, the King forbade the importation. of the com- modity, nor were private persons allowed to make it for the use of their families. Any breach of this command was punished by heavy penalties. Officials were appointed to protect the interests of the company,. and consumers may have been gratified to learn from a Royal proclamation that the soap made at Westminster was good, sweet, and serviceable for the use of his Majesty's lieges. THE WASTE OF SOAP.

This is an age of soaps, and the many varieties now manufactured would probably bewilder the makers in the time of James 1., whose productions were of the crudest kind compared with those of this century. House- wives, however, will certainly not be gratified to hear of the advance in price just announced of this indispensable commodity, although it is only five shillings per hundredweight. But this drawback might be converted into an advantage, if it should lead to a more careful consumption of the soap in daily use, for like mustard, as a rule, as much is wasted as is usefully consumed. If allowed to dry before- hand, and kept, as dry as possible when not in use, the life of a tablet of toilet soap may be doubled. For household purposes, soap is often lavishly and wastefully employed, and it is surprising how little of a good household soap will suffice to thoroughly cleanse articles in daily use, or garments washed at home.

THE TWO-STEP AND TANGO. An epidemic of dancing appears to have broken out this season, for every class of society is footing it merrily, either in the public parks, where an attempt is being made to revive the old country and other dances, or in exclusive "academies," where every- body in the social world is learning, or has learnt, the two-step and the tango, despite the condemnation of both by individuals and by moralists. Professors of these dances have this year been working as never before, and have found the greatest difficulty in keeping all their engagements fixed generally for the morning. Not for some time has a society fad been taken up with so much zest. Moreover, the Votaries of these dances are not all giddy young folks. The mothers accompany their daughters as pupils, the fathers their sons • and it is claimed that when properly danced there is not the slightest vulgarity in either the two-step or the tango, which are both graceful and fa s inating measures, although utterly unlike the minuet or any old-world dance. SUMMER BLOUSES.

Blouses are always a consideration at holiday times, and now that the sales are in full swing, bargains in these may be picked up in all the big shops. Some of the smartest of the new models are made of embroidered crepe de chine, combined with tulle, and finished with silk sashes in some brilliant colour, their ends decorated with silk fringe. But these are expensive, and for the average pocket the deliciously light con- fections of net and tulle are to be preferred. The frills in these blouses are not hemmed, but finished in the lightest possible manner by a machine invented specially for the purpose. Japanese silk is as much used as ever for garments of a useful kind to be worn with serge or cashmere skirts, and with a bow of black velvet ribbon are neat and always smart. THE COATEE OF THE MOMENT.

Among the latest novelties—and their name is legion—the little coatee of the moment deserves special mention, for it never came in prettier guise. It is a really artistic little garment and useful, too. In futurist-coloured and patterned silks it gives a quaint note to a simple dress, although the materials of which it is made may strike one as exaggerated in the highest degree. Fashions at the moment present striking contrasts, and the most elaborate toilettes are seen side by side with others of a severe simplicity in cut and make. For these latter, the little coatee, also simple in its lines and decorations, although gorgeous in colouring, is exactly suited. Fashions are all designed for the young and slim, and generally for the tall—but the quite elderly woman may indulge in a penchant for some- thing bright, which with a coatee of Cubist silk, will relieve a sombre toilette, without fear of reproach. This year's fashions lend themselves admirably to what may be termed elderly adaptations, and there is .no reason why the women approaching middle age, or

gair. V.?

even the frankly middle-aged, should not_ look as well turned out, and quite as sugar and fashionably dressed as a girl of twell't provided the former possesses the iudgillem that leads to a sensible adaptation of youth styles. urs THE CONTRASTING COAT AND SKIRT' cola The coat and skirt in contrasting gains in favour every day, and manY velf,„' - successful smart costumes of this tYPe 13,1" shown by the best houses.
